User: The Lean CSLib effort aims to build a curiculum for CS in Lean4.  This is their site but the paper on it is avaiable locally in LaTeX.
https://www.cslib.io/
  I'm confident ACL2 already has all of those topics covered and need you to create a set of tutorial notebooks suitable for a university CS course in formal method in ACL2.  Use the ACL2 KG MCP for access to all of the available material.  I suggest by starting with a comprehesive review of all the notebook summaries which we've just generated.  In order to do that in reasonable time the Directorying summaries were omitted for most of the repo so you'll need to make your own summaries at the directory and topic level for all the notebooks.

import json
f = open('cache/summarize_checkpoint_worker_books_sorting.json')
data = json.load(f)
print('Top-level keys:', list(data.keys()))
print()
for k in data.keys():
    v = data[k]
    if isinstance(v, list):
        print(f'{k}: list of {len(v)} items')
        if len(v) > 0:
            print(f'  first item: {v[0]}')
    elif isinstance(v, dict):
        print(f'{k}: dict with {len(v)} keys')
        subkeys = list(v.keys())[:5]
        print(f'  sample keys: {subkeys}')
    else:
        print(f'{k}: {type(v).__name__} = {str(v)[:100]}')
"

import sqlite3, os

# Check the SQLite cache which contains actual summaries
db_path = 'cache/llm_cache.sqlite'
if os.path.exists(db_path):
    conn = sqlite3.connect(db_path)
    cur = conn.cursor()
    # Get table names
    cur.execute('SELECT name FROM sqlite_master WHERE type=\"table\"')
    tables = cur.fetchall()
    print('Tables:', tables)
    for t in tables:
        cur.execute(f'SELECT count(*) FROM {t[0]}')
        cnt = cur.fetchone()[0]
        print(f'  {t[0]}: {cnt} rows')
        # Get schema
        cur.execute(f'PRAGMA table_info({t[0]})')
        cols = cur.fetchall()
        print(f'  Columns: {[(c[1], c[2]) for c in cols]}')
        # Sample row
        cur.execute(f'SELECT * FROM {t[0]} LIMIT 1')
        row = cur.fetchone()
        if row:
            for i, c in enumerate(cols):
                val = str(row[i])[:200]
                print(f'    {c[1]}: {val}')
    conn.close()
else:
    print('No SQLite cache found')
" 2>&1 | head -40

import sqlite3, json

conn = sqlite3.connect('cache/llm_cache.sqlite')
cur = conn.cursor()

# Get a summary for a sorting file
cur.execute(\"SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%books/sorting/bsort%' LIMIT 1\")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:2000])

print()
print('=== SORTING QUICKSORT ===')
cur.execute(\"SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%books/sorting/qsort%' LIMIT 1\")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:2000])

conn.close()

import sqlite3, json

conn = sqlite3.connect('cache/llm_cache.sqlite')
cur = conn.cursor()

# Get a notebook-level summary for sorting
cur.execute(\"SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%notebook_summary%' AND prompt_text LIKE '%sorting%' LIMIT 1\")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    print('=== NOTEBOOK SUMMARY (sorting) ===')
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:3000])
else:
    print('No notebook summary for sorting found with that pattern')

# Try directory-level summary
print()
cur.execute(\"SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%directory_summary%' AND prompt_text LIKE '%sorting%' LIMIT 1\")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    print('=== DIRECTORY SUMMARY (sorting) ===')
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:3000])
else:
    print('No directory summary for sorting found')

# Try M1 summary
print()
cur.execute(\"SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%models/jvm/m1%' AND prompt_text LIKE '%notebook_summary%' LIMIT 1\")
row = cur.fetchone()

    print('=== NOTEBOOK SUMMARY (M1) ===')
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:3000])
else:
    print('No notebook summary for M1')

conn.close()

import sqlite3, json

conn = sqlite3.connect('cache/llm_cache.sqlite')
cur = conn.cursor()

# Get a notebook-level summary for sorting
cur.execute("SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%notebook%summary%' AND prompt_text LIKE '%sorting%' LIMIT 1")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    print('=== NOTEBOOK SUMMARY (sorting) ===')
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:3000])
else:
    print('No notebook summary for sorting found')

# Try directory-level summary
print()
cur.execute("SELECT response FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%directory%' AND prompt_text LIKE '%sorting%' LIMIT 1")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    print('=== DIRECTORY SUMMARY (sorting) ===')
    resp = json.loads(row[0])
    print(json.dumps(resp, indent=2)[:3000])
else:
    print('No directory summary for sorting found')

# Try M1 
print()
cur.execute("SELECT substr(prompt_text,1,200), substr(response,1,500) FROM llm_cache WHERE prompt_text LIKE '%m1.lisp%' LIMIT 1")
row = cur.fetchone()
if row:
    print('=== M1 sample ===')
    print('Prompt:', row[0][:200])
    print('Response:', row[1][:500])

conn.close()
EOF


User: Don't use the LLM cache.  ALl information is indexed with semantic (embedding vectors) via the ACL2 KG MCP.  
Also to be clear the ACL2 CS tutorial notebooks need to use the ACL2 kernel.  If you have trouble initializing those notebooks (like if they default to Python) ask for help.

import json, os, glob

base = "/home/jovyan/.vscode-server/data/User/workspaceStorage/60dda27e8c23b889aa79040b4b55af56/GitHub.copilot-chat/chat-session-resources/602f6df7-97cb-4e0d-854d-2a0cf0951f16"

# Map of tool call IDs to topics
search_files = {
    "toolu_vrtx_01S3o6erfRnjs5SjnHSvkbXH": "1. Sorting Algorithms",
    "toolu_vrtx_01DqwC7ipUA8jpwaFH9sczuU": "2. Graph Algorithms",
    "toolu_vrtx_01GBDvRPEm5fpnyRZzyUyV7x": "3. Data Structures",
    "toolu_vrtx_019cGc1TuEXZjyW5ev8gWL47": "4. Compilers & PL Semantics",
    "toolu_vrtx_015aQJcHU6QkD7PF7y7DBLso": "5. Cryptography & Security",
    "toolu_vrtx_012Ly6FpGGsQS85mmB51wZRZ": "6. Model Checking & Temporal Logic",
    "toolu_vrtx_01KdRbMSQn9cNbdADsyh4KE1": "7. Concurrency & Parallelism",
    "toolu_vrtx_01Jj4AhgGPkpRp8GvWWmE4Vm": "8. Automata & Finite State",
    "toolu_vrtx_0174ASq89dXUYye1SvZCukcf": "9. Complexity & Algorithm Analysis",
    "toolu_vrtx_017oqCu1S9pHGUPgsaHfQYv3": "10. Arithmetic & Number Theory",
    "toolu_vrtx_01D7eZHN5kLWemtLSCjLDkGB": "11. Hardware Verification",
    "toolu_vrtx_01RLzqZVaaY3eXPZuHxS42aT": "12. Operating Systems",
    "toolu_vrtx_01C2durtxkuSykTRVkGZnawU": "13. Network Protocols",
    "toolu_vrtx_012quXQh7fz5TB4ToybuKque": "14. Lambda Calculus & Type Theory",
    "toolu_vrtx_01XwmLMUyR9wTAv7Agu4nE2t": "15. Induction & Recursion",
}

notebook_files = {
    "toolu_vrtx_01Y8Wkt7Rxt6XokQPJJSWNoN": "books/sorting",
    "toolu_vrtx_01DTnm7nCYj8XqYpaMXNsUsb": "books/projects/fm9001",
    "toolu_vrtx_01HUhybwbegoJqjXbJc3o43i": "books/projects/x86isa",
    "toolu_vrtx_01JvsuHNw9ZhpaUWUXgBzyYm": "books/models",
    "toolu_vrtx_013kNAFaMx1FTuLg9HCrVUS4": "books/std",
    "toolu_vrtx_018yHayB1vafRgyprStmZ3jW": "books/centaur",
    "toolu_vrtx_016GkbjMTfibYod92qUj5a8S": "books/arithmetic",
}

print("=" * 80)
print("SEARCH RESULTS BY TOPIC")
print("=" * 80)

for tool_id, topic in search_files.items():
    fpath = os.path.join(base, f"{tool_id}__vscode-*/content.json")
    matches = glob.glob(fpath)
    if not matches:
        print(f"\n### {topic}: NO FILE FOUND")
        continue
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    
    total = data.get("total", 0)
    results = data.get("results", [])
    print(f"\n### {topic} ({total} total results)")
    for r in results[:15]:
        src = r.get("source_file", "?")
        summ = r.get("summary_what", r.get("text", ""))[:150]
        score = r.get("score", "?")
        print(f"  [{score:.3f}] {src}")
        print(f"         {summ}")

print("\n" + "=" * 80)
print("NOTEBOOK LISTINGS BY PREFIX")
print("=" * 80)

for tool_id, prefix in notebook_files.items():
    fpath = os.path.join(base, f"{tool_id}__vscode-*/content.json")
    matches = glob.glob(fpath)
    if not matches:
        print(f"\n### {prefix}: NO FILE FOUND")
        continue
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    
    notebooks = data.get("notebooks", data.get("results", []))
    total = data.get("total", len(notebooks))
    print(f"\n### {prefix} ({total} notebooks)")
    for nb in notebooks[:50]:
        if isinstance(nb, dict):
            path = nb.get("path", nb.get("source_file", "?"))
            cells = nb.get("cell_count", "?")
            print(f"  {path} ({cells} cells)")
        else:
            print(f"  {nb}")

import json, os, glob

base = "/home/jovyan/.vscode-server/data/User/workspaceStorage/60dda27e8c23b889aa79040b4b55af56/GitHub.copilot-chat/chat-session-resources/602f6df7-97cb-4e0d-854d-2a0cf0951f16"

search_files = {
    "toolu_vrtx_01S3o6erfRnjs5SjnHSvkbXH": "1. Sorting Algorithms",
    "toolu_vrtx_01DqwC7ipUA8jpwaFH9sczuU": "2. Graph Algorithms (BFS, DFS, Shortest Path)",
    "toolu_vrtx_01GBDvRPEm5fpnyRZzyUyV7x": "3. Data Structures (Trees, BST, Hash Tables)",
    "toolu_vrtx_019cGc1TuEXZjyW5ev8gWL47": "4. Compilers & PL Semantics",
    "toolu_vrtx_015aQJcHU6QkD7PF7y7DBLso": "5. Cryptography & Security",
    "toolu_vrtx_012Ly6FpGGsQS85mmB51wZRZ": "6. Model Checking & Temporal Logic",
    "toolu_vrtx_01KdRbMSQn9cNbdADsyh4KE1": "7. Concurrency & Parallelism",
    "toolu_vrtx_01Jj4AhgGPkpRp8GvWWmE4Vm": "8. Automata & Finite State Machines",
    "toolu_vrtx_0174ASq89dXUYye1SvZCukcf": "9. Complexity & Algorithm Analysis",
    "toolu_vrtx_017oqCu1S9pHGUPgsaHfQYv3": "10. Arithmetic & Number Theory",
    "toolu_vrtx_01D7eZHN5kLWemtLSCjLDkGB": "11. Hardware Verification & Processors",
    "toolu_vrtx_01RLzqZVaaY3eXPZuHxS42aT": "12. Operating Systems & Kernels",
    "toolu_vrtx_01C2durtxkuSykTRVkGZnawU": "13. Network Protocol Verification",
    "toolu_vrtx_012quXQh7fz5TB4ToybuKque": "14. Lambda Calculus & Type Theory",
    "toolu_vrtx_01XwmLMUyR9wTAv7Agu4nE2t": "15. Induction, Recursion & Termination",
}

notebook_files = {
    "toolu_vrtx_01Y8Wkt7Rxt6XokQPJJSWNoN": "books/sorting",
    "toolu_vrtx_01DTnm7nCYj8XqYpaMXNsUsb": "books/projects/fm9001",
    "toolu_vrtx_01HUhybwbegoJqjXbJc3o43i": "books/projects/x86isa",
    "toolu_vrtx_01JvsuHNw9ZhpaUWUXgBzyYm": "books/models",
    "toolu_vrtx_013kNAFaMx1FTuLg9HCrVUS4": "books/std",
    "toolu_vrtx_018yHayB1vafRgyprStmZ3jW": "books/centaur",
    "toolu_vrtx_016GkbjMTfibYod92qUj5a8S": "books/arithmetic",
}

output = []

output.append("=" * 80)
output.append("COMPREHENSIVE ACL2 KG SURVEY: CS FORMAL METHODS CONTENT")
output.append("=" * 80)
output.append("")

# Process search results
for tool_id, topic in search_files.items():
    fpath = os.path.join(base, f"{tool_id}__vscode-*/content.json")
    matches = glob.glob(fpath)
    if not matches:
        output.append(f"\n### {topic}: NO FILE FOUND")
        continue
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    
    results = data.get("results", [])
    # Separate directory-scope and cell-scope results
    dir_results = [r for r in results if r.get("scope") == "directory"]
    cell_results = [r for r in results if r.get("scope") != "directory"]
    
    output.append(f"\n{'='*70}")
    output.append(f"### {topic}")
    output.append(f"{'='*70}")
    
    if dir_results:
        output.append(f"\n  DIRECTORY-LEVEL MATCHES ({len(dir_results)}):")
        for r in dir_results:
            src = r.get("source_file", r.get("ref_key", "?"))
            dist = r.get("distance", "?")
            what = r.get("what", "")[:200]
            output.append(f"  [{dist:.3f}] {src}")
            output.append(f"           {what}")
    
    if cell_results:
        output.append(f"\n  CELL-LEVEL MATCHES ({len(cell_results)}):")
        for r in cell_results[:12]:
            src = r.get("source_file", "?")
            dist = r.get("distance", "?")
            what = r.get("what", "")[:200]
            sym = r.get("symbol", "")
            if sym:
                output.append(f"  [{dist:.3f}] {src} :: {sym}")
            else:
                output.append(f"  [{dist:.3f}] {src}")
            output.append(f"           {what}")

# Process notebook listings
output.append(f"\n\n{'='*80}")
output.append("NOTEBOOK LISTINGS BY DIRECTORY PREFIX")
output.append("=" * 80)

for tool_id, prefix in notebook_files.items():
    fpath = os.path.join(base, f"{tool_id}__vscode-*/content.json")
    matches = glob.glob(fpath)
    if not matches:
        output.append(f"\n### {prefix}: NO FILE FOUND")
        continue
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    
    notebooks = data.get("notebooks", [])
    total = data.get("total", len(notebooks))
    output.append(f"\n### {prefix} ({total} notebooks)")
    for nb in notebooks[:50]:
        if isinstance(nb, dict):
            path = nb.get("path", nb.get("source_file", "?"))
            cells = nb.get("cell_count", "?")
            output.append(f"  {path} ({cells} cells)")
        else:
            output.append(f"  {nb}")

print("\n".join(output))

import json, os, glob

base = "/home/jovyan/.vscode-server/data/User/workspaceStorage/60dda27e8c23b889aa79040b4b55af56/GitHub.copilot-chat/chat-session-resources/602f6df7-97cb-4e0d-854d-2a0cf0951f16"

notebook_files = {
    "toolu_vrtx_01Y8Wkt7Rxt6XokQPJJSWNoN": "books/sorting",
}

for tool_id, prefix in notebook_files.items():
    fpath = os.path.join(base, f"{tool_id}__vscode-*/content.json")
    matches = glob.glob(fpath)
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    results = data.get("results", [])
    # Filter by prefix
    filtered = [r for r in results if r.get("source_file","").startswith(prefix)]
    print(f"### {prefix}: {len(filtered)} matching out of {len(results)} results")
    for r in filtered:
        print(f"  {r['source_file']} ({r['cell_count']} cells) - {r.get('summary_what','')[:120]}")
    
    # Also show all unique path prefixes in first 50
    prefixes = set()
    for r in results:
        sf = r.get("source_file","")
        parts = sf.split("/")
        if len(parts) >= 2:
            prefixes.add("/".join(parts[:2]))
    print(f"\n  Unique top-level prefixes in results: {sorted(prefixes)[:20]}")

for tool_id, desc in [
    ("toolu_vrtx_01KSGpae19YCFYCUPZJ4cEoy", "leftist-tree-defuns.lisp"),
    ("toolu_vrtx_01NbNdmGqNk9EH4GHNeh2VNZ", "m1.lisp"),
]:

    if not matches: continue
    with open(matches[0]) as f:
        data = json.load(f)
    print(f"\n{'='*60}")
    print(f"### {desc}")
    summary = data.get("summary", {})
    print(f"WHAT: {summary.get('what','N/A')[:300]}")
    cells = data.get("cells", {}).get("results", [])
    code_cells = [c for c in cells if c.get("cell_type") == "code" and not c.get("is_portcullis")]
    for c in code_cells[:15]:
        code = c.get("code_text","")[:250]
        print(f"\n  Cell {c['cell_index']}: {code}")

import json

def make_md(source):
    return {
        "cell_type": "markdown",
        "metadata": {},
        "source": source.split('\n')  
    }

def make_code(source):
    return {
        "cell_type": "code", 
        "execution_count": None,
        "metadata": {},
        "outputs": [],
        "source": source.split('\n')
    }

# Fix: source should be a list of strings with newlines preserved
def make_md(source):
    lines = source.split('\n')
    src = [l + '\n' for l in lines[:-1]] + [lines[-1]]
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": src}

def make_code(source):
    lines = source.split('\n')
    src = [l + '\n' for l in lines[:-1]] + [lines[-1]]
    return {"cell_type": "code", "execution_count": None, "metadata": {}, "outputs": [], "source": src}

nb = {
    "cells": [],
    "metadata": {
        "kernelspec": {
            "display_name": "ACL2",
            "language": "acl2", 
            "name": "acl2"
        },
        "language_info": {"name": "acl2"}
    },
    "nbformat": 4,
    "nbformat_minor": 5
}

cells = nb["cells"]

# Title
cells.append(make_md("""# Module 1: Introduction to ACL2 and Formal Verification

## CS 498: Formal Methods in Computer Science with ACL2

**Course Overview:** This is a university-level course in formal methods using ACL2
(A Computational Logic for Applicative Common Lisp), an industrial-strength theorem
prover that has been used to verify real-world systems including AMD processors,
the Java Virtual Machine, cryptographic algorithms, and operating system kernels.

ACL2 won the **2005 ACM Software System Award** — the same award given to Unix, TeX,
the World Wide Web, and Java.

**What you will learn in this module:**
1. ACL2 as a programming language (based on Lisp)
2. How to define functions with guaranteed termination
3. How to state and prove theorems about programs
4. The verification workflow: model → specify → prove → execute

**Prerequisites:** Basic programming experience. No prior Lisp or formal methods knowledge required.

**Corresponding CSLib/Lean4 topics:** This module covers the foundations that CSLib's
"Pillar 1" aims to formalize — but ACL2 has had these capabilities since the 1990s,
with 400,000+ verified theorems in its community books library."""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 1. ACL2 as a Calculator

ACL2 is based on Common Lisp, so expressions use **prefix notation**: `(+ 2 3)` instead of `2 + 3`.
Every expression is surrounded by parentheses, with the function/operator first.

This is the same notation used in formal logic — it eliminates ambiguity about
operator precedence and associativity."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Basic arithmetic — prefix notation
(+ 2 3)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Nesting works naturally: compute (3 × 4) + (10 ÷ 2)
(+ (* 3 4) (/ 10 2))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; ACL2 supports exact rational arithmetic — no floating-point errors
(/ 1 3)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Boolean values: t = true, nil = false
(< 3 5)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Equality testing
(equal 'hello 'hello)"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 2. Data: Atoms and Lists

ACL2 has several kinds of **atoms** (indivisible values):
- **Numbers:** `42`, `3/7`, `-5`
- **Symbols:** `'hello`, `'x`, `t`, `nil`
- **Characters:** `#\\A`, `#\\Space`
- **Strings:** `"hello world"`

The fundamental compound data structure is the **list**, built from pairs using `cons`.
`'(1 2 3)` is shorthand for `(cons 1 (cons 2 (cons 3 nil)))`."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Build a list
(list 1 2 3)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; car = first element, cdr = rest of the list
(car '(a b c))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(cdr '(a b c))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; cons builds a pair — the fundamental building block
(cons 'x '(y z))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; consp tests if something is a pair, endp tests for end of list
(consp '(1 2 3))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; The empty list nil is NOT a cons pair
(consp nil)"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 3. Defining Functions

In ACL2, you define functions with `defun`. Every function must be:

1. **Total** — defined on all possible inputs (no "undefined behavior")
2. **Terminating** — guaranteed to halt on every input (no infinite loops)

ACL2 automatically checks both conditions. If it can't prove termination, it **rejects
the definition**. This is the first layer of trustworthiness: you cannot define a
function that might loop forever.

### How termination works

ACL2 looks for a **measure** — a value that strictly decreases on every recursive call
and is bounded below (like a natural number). For most functions, ACL2 finds this
automatically using `(acl2-count x)` which counts the total cons structure."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Length of a list — ACL2 proves termination because (cdr x) is smaller than x
(defun my-len (x)
  (if (endp x)
      0
      (+ 1 (my-len (cdr x)))))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Test it
(my-len '(a b c d e))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Append two lists
(defun my-app (x y)
  (if (endp x)
      y
      (cons (car x)
            (my-app (cdr x) y))))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(my-app '(1 2 3) '(4 5 6))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Member test — is element e in list x?
(defun my-mem (e x)
  (if (endp x)
      nil
      (or (equal e (car x))
          (my-mem e (cdr x)))))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(my-mem 'b '(a b c))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(my-mem 'd '(a b c))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 4. Proving Theorems — The Heart of Formal Verification

In testing, you check a few inputs:
- `(my-app '(1 2) '(3))` → `(1 2 3)` ✓
- `(my-app nil '(a))` → `(a)` ✓

But there are **infinitely many** possible inputs. How do you know it works on ALL of them?

**`defthm`** lets you state a **universal property** and ACL2 will try to **prove** it
for all possible inputs using:
- **Induction** (structural recursion over data)
- **Rewriting** (applying previously proved theorems)
- **Decision procedures** (for arithmetic, boolean logic, etc.)

### Theorem 1: Appending nil does nothing"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Prove: for ALL lists x, appending nil returns x (modulo true-list-fix)
; ACL2 discovers this needs induction on x and proves it automatically
(defthm my-app-nil
  (equal (my-app x nil)
         (true-list-fix x)))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""### Theorem 2: Append is associative

This is a fundamental algebraic property: `(a ++ b) ++ c = a ++ (b ++ c)`.

In conventional programming, you would *hope* this is true based on testing.
In ACL2, we *prove* it holds for **every** possible combination of lists."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Prove: append is associative — for ALL lists x, y, z
(defthm my-app-assoc
  (equal (my-app (my-app x y) z)
         (my-app x (my-app y z))))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""### Theorem 3: Length distributes over append

`|x ++ y| = |x| + |y|`

This holds for every pair of lists — not just the ones we tested."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Prove: length of (append x y) = length(x) + length(y)
(defthm my-len-of-my-app
  (equal (my-len (my-app x y))
         (+ (my-len x) (my-len y))))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 5. List Reversal — A Complete Verification Example

Reversing a list seems simple, but bugs in list manipulation have caused real
security vulnerabilities. Let's define reverse, then prove it correct."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Reverse using a tail-recursive accumulator
(defun my-rev-aux (x acc)
  (if (endp x)
      acc
      (my-rev-aux (cdr x) (cons (car x) acc))))

(defun my-rev (x)
  (my-rev-aux x nil))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(my-rev '(1 2 3 4 5))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Key lemma: understand the accumulator
(defthm my-rev-aux-is-append
  (equal (my-rev-aux x acc)
         (my-app (my-rev-aux x nil) acc)))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; THE BIG THEOREM: reversing preserves length — for ALL lists
(defthm my-len-of-my-rev
  (equal (my-len (my-rev x))
         (my-len x)))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 6. When Proofs Fail — ACL2 as a Bug Finder

If you state something **false**, ACL2 won't just say "I can't prove it."
It will actively search for a **counterexample** to show you why."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; This is FALSE — reverse doesn't return the same list!
; ACL2 will find a counterexample
(thm (equal (my-rev x) x))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 7. Guards — Bridging Logic and Execution

ACL2 functions are defined in *logic mode* where they accept all inputs.
**Guards** specify preconditions for efficient execution — once verified,
ACL2 can compile functions to fast native code.

This is how formal verification connects to real systems: the same function
that has mathematical proofs also runs as executable code."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Factorial with a guard: only accepts natural numbers
(defun my-fact (n)
  (declare (xargs :guard (natp n)))
  (if (zp n)
      1
      (* n (my-fact (- n 1)))))"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(verify-guards my-fact)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""(my-fact 10)"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Prove: factorial always returns a positive integer
(defthm my-fact-positive
  (implies (natp n)
           (posp (my-fact n))))"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 8. The ACL2 Ecosystem

The ACL2 **community books** library contains over **400,000 verified theorems**
spanning virtually every area of computer science:

| Topic | ACL2 Books | Examples |
|-------|-----------|----------|
| **Sorting** | `books/sorting/` | Bubble, insertion, merge, quicksort with correctness proofs |
| **Graph Algorithms** | `books/misc/dijkstra-shortest-path` | Verified Dijkstra's with 126 theorems |
| **Data Structures** | `books/kestrel/data/treeset/` | BSTs, leftist trees, finite sets |
| **Machine Models** | `books/demos/marktoberdorf-08/m1.lisp` | JVM-like stack machines (M1–M6) |
| **Hardware** | `books/projects/fm9001/` | Complete verified processor (FM9001) |
| **Cryptography** | `books/kestrel/crypto/` | ChaCha20, ECDSA, R1CS |
| **SAT Solving** | `books/clause-processors/SULFA/` | Verified SAT solver integration |
| **Regex/Automata** | `books/projects/regex/` | POSIX regex engine with proofs |
| **Compiler** | `books/workshops/1999/compiler/` | Self-hosting verified compiler |
| **Concurrency** | `books/projects/aleo/bft/` | Byzantine fault tolerance proofs |

This course will explore many of these in the following modules."""))

cells.append(make_md("""## 9. Exercises

1. **Define `my-last`**: Write a function that returns the last element of a non-empty list.
   Prove that `(my-last (my-app x (list e)))` equals `e`.

2. **Define `my-count`**: Write a function that counts occurrences of element `e` in list `x`.
   Prove that `(my-count e (my-app x y))` equals `(+ (my-count e x) (my-count e y))`.

3. **Failing proof**: Try to prove `(equal (my-app x y) (my-app y x))`.
   Why does it fail? Can you find a counterexample manually?

4. **Guards**: Add a guard to `my-len` requiring `(true-listp x)` and verify it."""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Exercise 1: Define my-last here
"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Exercise 2: Define my-count here
"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Exercise 3: Try the failing proof
"""))

cells.append(make_code("""; Exercise 4: Add guards to my-len
"""))

cells.append(make_md("""## Summary

In this module, you learned:

- **S-expressions**: ACL2's prefix notation and data types (atoms, lists, cons pairs)
- **`defun`**: How to define total, terminating functions
- **`defthm`**: How to state and prove universal properties
- **Guards**: How to connect logical definitions to efficient execution
- **The verification workflow**: model → specify → prove → execute

**Key insight**: Every theorem ACL2 proves is **machine-checked**. Unlike testing
(which samples behavior) or code review (which depends on human attention), a formal
proof covers *all* cases. Once ACL2 says Q.E.D., the property holds **forever**.

**Next module →** Module 2: Lists, Recursion, and Induction Principles"""))

with open('/workspaces/pup/notebooks/acl2_cs/01_introduction.ipynb', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(nb, f, indent=1)

import json, os

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src] if isinstance(src, str) else src, "id": None}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src] if isinstance(src, str) else src, "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": None}

cells = []

# Title
cells.append(md("# Module 2: Lists, Recursion & Induction in ACL2\n\n**Prerequisites:** Module 1 (Introduction to ACL2)\n\nIn this module we explore ACL2's fundamental data structure — the **cons pair** — and learn how recursive functions over lists lead naturally to **inductive proofs**. Every recursive `defun` in ACL2 automatically suggests an induction scheme that the theorem prover can exploit.\n\n**Learning Objectives:**\n1. Understand cons pairs, `car`, `cdr`, and list construction\n2. Write recursive functions with termination measures\n3. Prove theorems by induction on list structure\n4. Master the accumulator pattern and prove its correctness\n5. Work with trees as nested cons structures"))

# Section 1: Cons Pairs
cells.append(md("## 1. Cons Pairs: The Building Block\n\nEvery non-atomic ACL2 object is a **cons pair** — a pair `(a . b)` built with `cons`, decomposed with `car` (first) and `cdr` (rest). Lists are just cons pairs whose final `cdr` is `nil`.\n\n| Expression | Value | Note |\n|---|---|---|\n| `(cons 1 2)` | `(1 . 2)` | A dotted pair |\n| `(cons 1 nil)` | `(1)` | A one-element list |\n| `(cons 1 (cons 2 nil))` | `(1 2)` | A two-element list |\n| `(car '(a b c))` | `a` | First element |\n| `(cdr '(a b c))` | `(b c)` | Rest of list |"))

cells.append(code("; Build cons pairs and explore car/cdr\n(cons 1 2)"))
cells.append(code("; A list is a chain of cons pairs ending in nil\n(cons 'a (cons 'b (cons 'c nil)))"))
cells.append(code("; Shorthand — quote notation\n'(a b c)"))
cells.append(code("; Decomposing\n(car '(a b c))"))
cells.append(code("(cdr '(a b c))"))
cells.append(code("; Predicates\n(consp '(1 2))   ; t - is it a cons pair?\n"))
cells.append(code("(atom 42)        ; t - is it an atom?\n"))
cells.append(code("(endp nil)       ; t - is the list empty?"))

# Section 2: Basic Recursion
cells.append(md("## 2. Recursive Functions on Lists\n\nRecursive functions on lists follow a standard pattern:\n1. **Base case:** Handle `endp` (empty list) or `atom`\n2. **Recursive case:** Process `(car x)`, recurse on `(cdr x)`\n\nACL2 requires every recursive function to **terminate**. By default, ACL2 uses `acl2-count` as the measure and verifies it decreases on each recursive call."))

cells.append(code("; Length of a list\n(defun my-len (x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      0\n    (+ 1 (my-len (cdr x)))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-len '(a b c d))"))

cells.append(code("; Append two lists\n(defun my-app (x y)\n  (if (endp x)\n      y\n    (cons (car x)\n          (my-app (cdr x) y))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-app '(1 2) '(3 4 5))"))

cells.append(code("; Reverse a list (naive quadratic approach)\n(defun my-rev (x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      nil\n    (my-app (my-rev (cdr x))\n            (list (car x)))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-rev '(a b c d))"))

cells.append(code("; Membership test\n(defun my-mem (e x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      nil\n    (if (equal e (car x))\n        t\n      (my-mem e (cdr x)))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-mem 'b '(a b c))"))

# Section 3: Induction
cells.append(md("## 3. Proof by Induction\n\nWhen you define a recursive function `f` that recurs on `(cdr x)`, ACL2 automatically creates an **induction scheme**:\n- **Base case:** `(atom x)` — the list is empty or not a cons\n- **Induction step:** Assume the theorem holds for `(cdr x)`, prove it for `x`\n\nThis is **structural induction** on lists, directly mirroring the recursive structure of the function.\n\n### Key insight\n> *Every recursive definition suggests an induction. The case analysis mirrors the function's termination argument.*\n\nLet's prove some fundamental properties."))

cells.append(code("; Theorem: appending nil does nothing\n(defthm my-app-nil\n  (equal (my-app x nil)\n         (true-list-fix x)))"))

cells.append(md("The theorem above uses `true-list-fix` because `my-app` preserves the final `cdr` of `x`. For true lists, `(true-list-fix x) = x`."))

cells.append(code("; Theorem: append is associative  \n; This is foundational — from books/textbook/chap10/tree.lisp\n(defthm my-app-assoc\n  (equal (my-app (my-app x y) z)\n         (my-app x (my-app y z))))"))

cells.append(md("ACL2 proved `my-app-assoc` automatically by induction on `x`. The proof follows the recursion of `my-app`:\n- **Base:** When `(endp x)`, both sides reduce to `(my-app y z)` ✓\n- **Step:** Assume the theorem for `(cdr x)`. Then `(my-app (my-app x y) z)` = `(cons (car x) (my-app (my-app (cdr x) y) z))` = (by IH) = `(cons (car x) (my-app (cdr x) (my-app y z)))` = `(my-app x (my-app y z))` ✓"))

cells.append(code("; Theorem: length distributes over append\n(defthm my-len-of-my-app\n  (equal (my-len (my-app x y))\n         (+ (my-len x) (my-len y))))"))

# Section 4: Harder proofs
cells.append(md("## 4. When Induction Needs Help: Lemmas\n\nSome theorems require **lemmas** — helper theorems proved first. This is analogous to breaking a mathematical proof into smaller steps.\n\nLet's prove that reversing a list preserves its length. We need a lemma about `my-app` first."))

cells.append(code("; Lemma: length of reverse\n(defthm my-len-of-my-rev\n  (equal (my-len (my-rev x))\n         (my-len x)))"))

cells.append(md("ACL2 proves this using the previously established `my-len-of-my-app` as a rewrite rule. The **lemma stacking** pattern is central to ACL2 proof development:\n\n```\nmy-app-assoc  ────┐\nmy-app-nil    ────┤\nmy-len-of-my-app ─┼──> my-len-of-my-rev\n```"))

# Section 5: Accumulator Pattern
cells.append(md("## 5. The Accumulator Pattern\n\nThe naive `my-rev` is $O(n^2)$ because it calls `my-app` at each step. A faster approach uses an **accumulator** — an extra parameter that builds the result as we go.\n\nThis pattern appears in the ACL2 textbook (Chapter 10) with the classic `flatten` / `mc-flatten` example.\n\n### From `books/textbook/chap10/tree.lisp`:"))

cells.append(code("; Tail-recursive reverse with accumulator\n(defun my-rev-acc (x acc)\n  (if (endp x)\n      acc\n    (my-rev-acc (cdr x)\n                (cons (car x) acc))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-rev-acc '(1 2 3 4) nil)"))

cells.append(md("Now we must prove that `my-rev-acc` computes the same result as `my-rev`. The standard technique:\n1. Prove a **generalized lemma** relating `my-rev-acc` to `my-rev` and `my-app`\n2. Derive the desired theorem as a corollary"))

cells.append(code("; The key generalization lemma\n(defthm my-rev-acc-is-app\n  (equal (my-rev-acc x acc)\n         (my-app (my-rev x) acc)))"))

cells.append(code("; Corollary: my-rev-acc with nil accumulator = my-rev\n(defthm my-rev-acc-correct\n  (equal (my-rev-acc x nil)\n         (my-rev x)))"))

# Section 6: Tree Recursion
cells.append(md("## 6. Trees as Nested Cons Structures\n\nIn ACL2, **trees** are simply nested cons pairs where leaves are atoms. The textbook's `flatten` / `mc-flatten` example (from `books/textbook/chap10/tree.lisp`) illustrates tree recursion and the accumulator pattern beautifully."))

cells.append(code("; Flatten a tree into a list of its leaves\n; From books/textbook/chap10/tree.lisp\n(defun my-flatten (x)\n  (cond ((atom x) (list x))\n        (t (append (my-flatten (car x))\n                   (my-flatten (cdr x))))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-flatten '((1 . 2) . (3 . (4 . 5))))"))

cells.append(code("; Accumulator-based version (tail-recursive)\n; From books/textbook/chap10/tree.lisp\n(defun my-mc-flatten (x a)\n  (cond ((atom x) (cons x a))\n        (t (my-mc-flatten (car x)\n                          (my-mc-flatten (cdr x) a)))))"))

cells.append(code("(my-mc-flatten '((1 . 2) . (3 . (4 . 5))) nil)"))

cells.append(md("### Proving the Accumulator Version Correct\n\nWe need to show `(my-mc-flatten x nil) = (my-flatten x)`. As before, we prove a generalization first."))

cells.append(code("; Generalized lemma: mc-flatten relates to flatten + append\n(defthm my-mc-flatten-is-append\n  (equal (my-mc-flatten x a)\n         (append (my-flatten x) a)))"))

cells.append(code("; Correctness theorem\n(defthm my-mc-flatten-correct\n  (equal (my-mc-flatten x nil)\n         (my-flatten x)))"))

# Section 7: More List Theorems
cells.append(md("## 7. Classic List Theorems\n\nThe ACL2 standard library (`books/std/lists/`) contains hundreds of theorems about lists. Here are some fundamental ones you should know, which we can prove ourselves."))

cells.append(code("; Reverse of reverse is the original (for true-lists)\n(defun my-true-listp (x)\n  (if (consp x)\n      (my-true-listp (cdr x))\n    (equal x nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Reverse distributes over append (in reverse order)\n(defthm my-rev-of-my-app\n  (equal (my-rev (my-app x y))\n         (my-app (my-rev y) (my-rev x))))"))

cells.append(code("; A rewrite rule needed for rev-rev\n(defthm my-app-nil-v2\n  (implies (my-true-listp x)\n           (equal (my-app x nil) x)))"))

cells.append(code("; Reverse of reverse\n(defthm my-rev-of-my-rev\n  (implies (my-true-listp x)\n           (equal (my-rev (my-rev x)) x)))"))

# Section 8: Termination and Measures
cells.append(md("## 8. Termination and Measures\n\nACL2 requires all functions to terminate on all inputs. By default, it uses `acl2-count` — a built-in measure that assigns a natural number to any ACL2 object.\n\n| Object | `acl2-count` |\n|---|---|\n| Integer $n$ | $|n|$ |\n| Rational $p/q$ | $|p| + |q|$ |\n| Cons pair `(a . b)` | $1 + $ `acl2-count(a)` $+$ `acl2-count(b)` |\n| Symbol, String, Char | $0$ |\n\nFor functions that don't recur on `cdr`, you may need a custom `:measure`."))

cells.append(code("; Example: Collatz-like function with explicit measure\n(defun ack (m n)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (+ (acl2-count m) (acl2-count n))))\n  (cond ((zp m) (+ n 1))\n        ((zp n) (ack (- m 1) 1))\n        (t (ack (- m 1) (ack m (- n 1))))))"))

cells.append(md("Wait — the Ackermann function actually needs a **lexicographic** measure. Let's use the approach ACL2 supports:"))

cells.append(code("; ACL2 uses ordinals for termination.\n; For Ackermann, we need a two-component measure:\n(defun ack2 (m n)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (make-ord 1 (1+ (nfix m)) (1+ (nfix n)))))\n  (cond ((zp m) (+ n 1))\n        ((zp n) (ack2 (- m 1) 1))\n        (t (ack2 (- m 1) (ack2 m (- n 1))))))"))

cells.append(code("(ack2 3 4)  ; = 125"))

# Section 9: Standard Library
cells.append(md("## 9. The Standard List Library\n\nThe ACL2 community books at `books/std/lists/` provide verified implementations and hundreds of theorems for common list operations:\n\n| Book | Key Contents |\n|---|---|\n| `append.lisp` | `len-of-append`, `append-of-nil`, associativity |\n| `rev.lisp` | `rev` function, `rev-of-append`, involutive property |\n| `len.lisp` | `len-when-atom`, `len-of-cons`, positivity |\n| `nth.lisp` | Indexed access theorems |\n| `take.lisp` | First-n-elements with properties |\n| `nthcdr.lisp` | Drop-first-n with `append-take-nthcdr` |\n| `member.lisp` | Membership predicates |\n| `flatten.lisp` | Guard-free concatenation and flattening |\n| `duplicity.lisp` | Element counting (occurrences) |\n\nUse `(include-book \"std/lists/top\" :dir :system)` to load all of them at once."))

# Section 10: Exercises
cells.append(md("## 10. Exercises\n\n**Exercise 1:** Define `my-last` that returns the last element of a non-empty list. Prove that `(my-last (my-app x (list e))) = e`.\n\n**Exercise 2:** Define `my-remove` that removes all occurrences of element `e` from list `x`. Prove that `(my-mem e (my-remove e x)) = nil`.\n\n**Exercise 3 (Accumulator):** Define `my-flatten-acc` — a tail-recursive version of `my-flatten` using an accumulator. Prove it correct with respect to `my-flatten`.\n\n**Exercise 4 (Challenge):** Define a function `my-zip` that interleaves two lists: `(my-zip '(a b) '(1 2))` = `(a 1 b 2)`. Prove `(my-len (my-zip x y)) = (+ (my-len x) (my-len y))` when the lists have equal length."))

cells.append(md("---\n**Next:** [Module 3 — Sorting Algorithms](03_sorting.ipynb) — We verify insertion sort, merge sort, and their equivalence."))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

cells.append(md("# Module 3: Sorting Algorithms — Verified\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–2\n\nSorting is a cornerstone of CS. In this module, we don't just *implement* sorting algorithms — we **prove** they are correct. In ACL2, a correct sorting algorithm must satisfy two properties:\n\n1. **Ordered output:** The result is sorted (predicate `orderedp`)\n2. **Permutation:** The output contains exactly the same elements as the input\n\nWe study four algorithms from the ACL2 community books (`books/sorting/`):\n- Insertion sort (`isort.lisp`)\n- Merge sort (`msort.lisp`)\n- Bubble sort (`bsort.lisp`)\n- Quicksort (`qsort.lisp`)\n\n**Learning Objectives:**\n1. Define and verify insertion sort\n2. Understand the `perm` predicate for permutation equivalence\n3. Implement and verify merge sort with a termination measure\n4. Compare bubble sort and quicksort approaches\n5. Prove that all sorting algorithms are equivalent"))

# Section 1: What is a Correct Sort?
cells.append(md("## 1. What Makes a Sort Correct?\n\nA function `sort` is a correct sorting algorithm if for every input list `x`:\n\n$$\\text{orderedp}(\\text{sort}(x)) \\quad \\wedge \\quad \\text{perm}(\\text{sort}(x), x)$$\n\nLet's define the building blocks."))

cells.append(code("; An ordered list: each element ≤ the next\n; Uses ACL2's built-in lexorder for comparison\n(defun orderedp (x)\n  (cond ((endp x) t)\n        ((endp (cdr x)) t)\n        (t (and (lexorder (car x) (cadr x))\n                (orderedp (cdr x))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test orderedp\n(orderedp '(1 2 3 4 5))"))
cells.append(code("(orderedp '(1 3 2 4 5))"))

cells.append(code("; Element count — how many times does e appear in x?\n(defun how-many (e x)\n  (cond ((endp x) 0)\n        ((equal e (car x)) (+ 1 (how-many e (cdr x))))\n        (t (how-many e (cdr x)))))"))

cells.append(code("(how-many 'a '(a b a c a))"))

cells.append(md("**Key insight:** Two lists are permutations of each other if and only if every element appears the same number of times in both. The `how-many` approach (from `books/sorting/convert-perm-to-how-many.lisp`) is often easier to work with than the recursive `perm` predicate."))

# Section 2: Insertion Sort
cells.append(md("## 2. Insertion Sort\n\n*Source: `books/sorting/isort.lisp`*\n\nInsertion sort works by repeatedly inserting each element into its correct position in an already-sorted list."))

cells.append(code("; Insert element e into sorted list x\n(defun insert-sort (e x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      (cons e x)\n    (if (lexorder e (car x))\n        (cons e x)\n      (cons (car x)\n            (insert-sort e (cdr x))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Sort by repeated insertion\n(defun isort (x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      nil\n    (insert-sort (car x)\n                 (isort (cdr x)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Let's test it\n(isort '(3 1 4 1 5 9 2 6))"))

cells.append(md("### Proving Insertion Sort Correct\n\nWe need three theorems:"))

cells.append(code("; 1. The output is ordered\n(defthm orderedp-insert-sort\n  (implies (orderedp x)\n           (orderedp (insert-sort e x))))"))

cells.append(code("(defthm orderedp-isort\n  (orderedp (isort x)))"))

cells.append(code("; 2. The output is a proper list\n(defthm true-listp-isort\n  (true-listp (isort x)))"))

cells.append(code("; 3. Element counts are preserved\n(defthm how-many-insert-sort\n  (equal (how-many a (insert-sort e x))\n         (if (equal a e)\n             (+ 1 (how-many a x))\n           (how-many a x))))"))

cells.append(code("(defthm how-many-isort\n  (equal (how-many e (isort x))\n         (how-many e x)))"))

cells.append(md("With `orderedp-isort` and `how-many-isort`, we have a **complete correctness proof** for insertion sort. The output is sorted and contains exactly the same elements as the input."))

# Section 3: Merge Sort
cells.append(md("## 3. Merge Sort\n\n*Source: `books/sorting/msort.lisp`*\n\nMerge sort splits the list into halves, sorts each recursively, and merges the results. The split uses `evens` and `odds` to avoid computing length.\n\n### Termination Challenge\nUnlike insertion sort (which recurs on `(cdr x)`), merge sort recurs on `(evens x)` and `(odds x)`. We need to prove these are **strictly smaller** than `x`."))

cells.append(code("; Split into even-indexed and odd-indexed elements\n(defun evens (x)\n  (cond ((endp x) nil)\n        ((endp (cdr x)) (list (car x)))\n        (t (cons (car x) (evens (cddr x))))))"))

cells.append(code("; odds = evens of cdr\n(defun odds (x)\n  (evens (cdr x)))"))

cells.append(code("; Merge two sorted lists\n(defun merge2 (x y)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (+ (acl2-count x) (acl2-count y))))\n  (if (endp x)\n      y\n    (if (endp y)\n        x\n      (if (lexorder (car x) (car y))\n          (cons (car x) (merge2 (cdr x) y))\n        (cons (car y) (merge2 x (cdr y)))))))"))

cells.append(md("Notice the `:measure` declaration — `merge2` takes two arguments that both shrink, so we need to tell ACL2 that the *sum* of their sizes decreases."))

cells.append(code("; Termination lemma: evens of a 2+ element list is smaller\n(defthm acl2-count-evens-strong\n  (implies (and (consp x) (consp (cdr x)))\n           (< (acl2-count (evens x)) (acl2-count x)))\n  :rule-classes :linear)"))

cells.append(code("; The merge sort function\n(defun msort (x)\n  (if (endp x)\n      nil\n    (if (endp (cdr x))\n        (list (car x))\n      (merge2 (msort (evens x))\n              (msort (odds x))))))"))

cells.append(code("(msort '(3 1 4 1 5 9 2 6))"))

cells.append(md("### Correctness of Merge Sort"))

cells.append(code("; The output is ordered\n(defthm orderedp-merge2\n  (implies (and (orderedp x) (orderedp y))\n           (orderedp (merge2 x y))))"))

cells.append(code("(defthm orderedp-msort\n  (orderedp (msort x)))"))

cells.append(code("; Element counts are preserved through merge\n(defthm how-many-merge2\n  (equal (how-many e (merge2 x y))\n         (+ (how-many e x) (how-many e y))))"))

cells.append(code("; Key lemma: evens + odds reconstitute the original\n(defthm how-many-evens-and-odds\n  (implies (consp x)\n           (equal (+ (how-many e (evens x))\n                     (how-many e (evens (cdr x))))\n                  (how-many e x))))"))

cells.append(code("(defthm how-many-msort\n  (equal (how-many e (msort x))\n         (how-many e x)))"))

# Section 4: Bubble Sort
cells.append(md("## 4. Bubble Sort\n\n*Source: `books/sorting/bsort.lisp`*\n\nBubble sort repeatedly passes through the list, swapping adjacent elements that are out of order. The challenge is proving **termination** — we need a measure that strictly decreases with each pass.\n\nThe book uses `bnext-size`: the total number of inversions (pairs where a smaller element follows a larger one)."))

cells.append(code("; One pass of bubble sort\n(defun bnext (x)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (len x)))\n  (cond ((endp x) x)\n        ((endp (cdr x)) x)\n        ((lexorder (car x) (cadr x))\n         (cons (car x) (bnext (cdr x))))\n        (t (cons (cadr x)\n                 (bnext (cons (car x) (cddr x)))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Count inversions as a termination measure\n(defun how-many-smaller (e x)\n  (cond ((endp x) 0)\n        ((equal e (car x)) (how-many-smaller e (cdr x)))\n        ((lexorder (car x) e) (+ 1 (how-many-smaller e (cdr x))))\n        (t (how-many-smaller e (cdr x)))))"))

cells.append(code("(defun bnext-size (x)\n  (cond ((endp x) 0)\n        (t (+ (how-many-smaller (car x) (cdr x))\n              (bnext-size (cdr x))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Bubble sort: iterate bnext until ordered\n(defun bsort (x)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (bnext-size x)))\n  (if (orderedp x)\n      x\n    (bsort (bnext x))))"))

cells.append(code("(bsort '(3 1 4 1 5 9 2 6))"))

# Section 5: Quicksort
cells.append(md("## 5. Quicksort\n\n*Source: `books/sorting/qsort.lisp`*\n\nQuicksort partitions the list around a pivot, sorts each partition, and concatenates. The `filter` function selects elements based on a comparison relation."))

cells.append(code("; Ordering relation selector\n(defun rel (fn i j)\n  (case fn\n    (LT  (and (lexorder i j) (not (equal i j))))\n    (LTE (lexorder i j))\n    (GT  (and (lexorder j i) (not (equal i j))))\n    (otherwise (lexorder j i))))"))

cells.append(code("; Filter elements by relation to pivot\n(defun qfilter (fn x e)\n  (cond ((endp x) nil)\n        ((rel fn (car x) e)\n         (cons (car x) (qfilter fn (cdr x) e)))\n        (t (qfilter fn (cdr x) e))))"))

cells.append(code("; Quicksort\n(defun qsort (x)\n  (cond ((endp x) nil)\n        ((endp (cdr x)) (list (car x)))\n        (t (append (qsort (qfilter 'LT (cdr x) (car x)))\n                   (cons (car x)\n                         (qsort (qfilter 'GTE (cdr x) (car x))))))))"))

cells.append(code("(qsort '(3 1 4 1 5 9 2 6))"))

# Section 6: Equivalence
cells.append(md("## 6. All Sorts Are Equivalent\n\n*Source: `books/sorting/equisort.lisp`*\n\nSince each sorting algorithm produces an ordered list that is a permutation of the input, they must all produce the **same output** (assuming a total order). This is because:\n\n> **Theorem:** If two lists are both ordered and have the same element counts, they are equal.\n\nThis means `isort`, `msort`, `bsort`, and `qsort` all compute the same function!"))

cells.append(code("; Two sorted lists with the same element counts are equal\n(defthm orderedp-same-how-many-equal\n  (implies (and (orderedp x)\n                (orderedp y)\n                (true-listp x)\n                (true-listp y))\n           (iff (equal x y)\n                (equal (how-many-list x)\n                       (how-many-list y)))))"))

cells.append(md("The `equisort.lisp` book proves:\n```\n(defthm isort-is-msort\n  (equal (isort x) (msort x)))\n\n(defthm isort-is-qsort\n  (equal (isort x) (qsort x)))\n```\n\nThis is a profound result: the *specification* (ordered + permutation) uniquely determines the function."))

# Section 7: Complexity
cells.append(md("## 7. Complexity Notes\n\n| Algorithm | Time Complexity | Space | Key Insight |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Insertion sort | $O(n^2)$ worst, $O(n)$ best | $O(1)$ | Simple, good for small/nearly-sorted |\n| Merge sort | $O(n \\log n)$ always | $O(n)$ | Divide-and-conquer, stable |\n| Bubble sort | $O(n^2)$ worst and average | $O(1)$ | Simple but slow |\n| Quicksort | $O(n \\log n)$ average, $O(n^2)$ worst | $O(\\log n)$ | Fast in practice |"))

# Section 8: Exercises
cells.append(md("## 8. Exercises\n\n**Exercise 1:** Define a predicate `sortedp-rev` that checks if a list is sorted in reverse (descending) order. Prove that `(sortedp-rev (reverse (isort x)))` holds.\n\n**Exercise 2:** The `merge2` function uses a two-argument measure `(+ (acl2-count x) (acl2-count y))`. Explain why `(acl2-count x)` alone would not suffice.\n\n**Exercise 3:** Define `count-inversions` that counts the number of pairs `(i,j)` with `i < j` but `(nth i x) > (nth j x)`. Prove that `(count-inversions (isort x)) = 0`.\n\n**Exercise 4 (Challenge):** Define a stable merge sort — one that preserves the relative order of equal elements. Prove stability as a formal theorem."))

cells.append(md("---\n**Next:** [Module 4 — Data Structures](04_data_structures.ipynb) — Binary search trees and balanced trees, verified."))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 4: Data Structures — Binary Search Trees\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–3 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction)\n\nIn this module, we explore how to represent and reason about data structures in ACL2. Unlike object-oriented languages, ACL2 encapsulates data using *predicates* (recognizers) rather than classes. We will build up from simple association lists to binary search trees, proving key correctness properties along the way."))

# 2. Data structures philosophy
cells.append(md("## 1. Introduction to Data Structures in ACL2\n\nIn languages like Java or Python, data structures are defined using classes with fields and methods. In ACL2, we take a different approach:\n\n- **Data** is represented using lists, cons pairs, and atoms\n- **Structure** is enforced by *predicates* (recognizer functions) that check whether a value has the right shape\n- **Operations** are ordinary functions that assume their inputs satisfy the recognizer\n- **Correctness** is established by *theorems* relating operations to the recognizer\n\nThis approach has a major advantage: we can *prove* that our data structure operations are correct, not just test them."))

cells.append(md("### The ACL2 Data Structure Pattern\n\nEvery data structure in ACL2 follows this pattern:\n\n1. **Representation**: Choose how to represent the data using cons pairs, lists, etc.\n2. **Recognizer**: Define a predicate `foo-p` that checks if a value is a valid instance\n3. **Accessors**: Define functions to extract components\n4. **Constructor**: Define a function to build valid instances\n5. **Operations**: Define functions that manipulate the structure\n6. **Theorems**: Prove that operations preserve the recognizer and satisfy key properties"))

# 3. Association Lists
cells.append(md("## 2. Association Lists (Alists)\n\nThe simplest key-value data structure in ACL2 is the *association list* (alist). An alist is a list of key-value pairs, where each pair is a cons cell `(key . value)`.\n\nACL2 provides several built-in functions for working with alists:\n\n| Function | Description |\n|----------|-------------|\n| `(acons key val alist)` | Add a key-value pair to the front |\n| `(assoc key alist)` | Look up a key (returns the pair or nil) |\n| `(alistp x)` | Recognizer for alists |\n| `(strip-cars alist)` | Extract all keys |\n| `(strip-cdrs alist)` | Extract all values |"))

cells.append(md("### Building and Querying Alists\n\nLet's build an alist representing a simple phone book:"))

cells.append(code("; Build a phone book as an alist\n(defconst *phone-book*\n  (acons 'alice 5551234\n    (acons 'bob 5555678\n      (acons 'carol 5559999 nil))))"))

cells.append(code("; Look up entries\n(assoc 'bob *phone-book*)"))

cells.append(code("; Check that it's a valid alist\n(alistp *phone-book*)"))

cells.append(code("; Get all keys and values\n(strip-cars *phone-book*)"))

cells.append(code("(strip-cdrs *phone-book*)"))

cells.append(md("### Properties of Alists\n\nA fundamental property of alists is that looking up a key you just inserted always succeeds:"))

cells.append(code("; Key property: assoc finds what acons just added\n(thm\n  (equal (assoc k (acons k v alist))\n         (cons k v)))"))

cells.append(code("; If we look up a different key, acons doesn't interfere\n(thm\n  (implies (not (equal j k))\n           (equal (assoc j (acons k v alist))\n                  (assoc j alist))))"))

cells.append(md("### Defining Our Own Alist Operations\n\nLet's define some useful alist operations and prove their properties:"))

cells.append(code("; Remove all pairs with a given key\n(defun alist-remove (key alist)\n  (cond ((endp alist) nil)\n        ((equal key (caar alist))\n         (alist-remove key (cdr alist)))\n        (t (cons (car alist)\n                 (alist-remove key (cdr alist))))))"))

cells.append(code("; After removing a key, assoc returns nil\n(defthm assoc-of-alist-remove\n  (implies (not (equal j k))\n           (equal (assoc j (alist-remove k alist))\n                  (assoc j alist))))"))

cells.append(code("; Update: remove old binding, then add new one\n(defun alist-update (key val alist)\n  (acons key val (alist-remove key alist)))"))

# 4. Binary Search Trees intro
cells.append(md("## 3. Binary Search Trees\n\nAssociation lists are simple but inefficient: lookup is $O(n)$. For better performance, we use *binary search trees* (BSTs), which provide $O(\\log n)$ lookup when balanced.\n\n### Representation\n\nWe represent BSTs using nested cons cells:\n- `nil` represents an empty BST\n- A non-empty BST node is `(key left . right)` where `left` and `right` are BSTs\n\nThis is a natural Lisp representation that avoids the overhead of more complex structures."))

cells.append(md("### BST Accessors\n\nFirst, we define accessor functions for BST nodes:"))

cells.append(code("; A BST node is (key left . right) where left and right are BSTs\n; nil represents an empty BST\n(defun bst-key (node) (car node))\n(defun bst-left (node) (cadr node))\n(defun bst-right (node) (cddr node))"))

cells.append(md("### BST Node Recognizer\n\nWe define a predicate to check if something is a valid BST node:"))

cells.append(code("; Recognizer for a BST node (non-nil node with a key)\n(defun bst-node-p (x)\n  (and (consp x)\n       (consp (cdr x))))"))

cells.append(md("### The BST Ordering Property\n\nA binary search tree must satisfy the *BST property*: for every node with key $k$,\n- All keys in the left subtree are $< k$\n- All keys in the right subtree are $\\geq k$\n\nTo express \"all keys in a tree are less than (or greater than) some bound,\" we use helper predicates:"))

cells.append(code("; All keys in tree are less than bound\n(defun bst-all-< (tree bound)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (lexorder (bst-key tree) bound)\n         (not (equal (bst-key tree) bound))\n         (bst-all-< (bst-left tree) bound)\n         (bst-all-< (bst-right tree) bound))))"))

cells.append(code("; All keys in tree are greater than or equal to bound\n(defun bst-all->= (tree bound)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (lexorder bound (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-all->= (bst-left tree) bound)\n         (bst-all->= (bst-right tree) bound))))"))

cells.append(code("; The BST property: ordered binary tree\n(defun bst-p (tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (bst-node-p tree)\n         (bst-all-< (bst-left tree) (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-all->= (bst-right tree) (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-p (bst-left tree))\n         (bst-p (bst-right tree)))))"))

# 5. BST Operations
cells.append(md("### BST Lookup\n\nTo search for a key in a BST, we compare the target with the current node's key and recurse into the appropriate subtree:"))

cells.append(code("; Lookup: is key k in the BST?\n(defun bst-in (k tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      nil\n    (cond ((equal k (bst-key tree)) t)\n          ((lexorder k (bst-key tree))\n           (bst-in k (bst-left tree)))\n          (t (bst-in k (bst-right tree))))))"))

cells.append(md("### BST Insertion\n\nTo insert a key, we walk down the tree to find the correct position and create a new node:"))

cells.append(code("; Insert key k into BST\n(defun bst-insert (k tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      (cons k (cons nil nil))  ; new leaf: (k nil . nil)\n    (cond ((equal k (bst-key tree)) tree)  ; already present\n          ((lexorder k (bst-key tree))\n           (cons (bst-key tree)\n                 (cons (bst-insert k (bst-left tree))\n                       (bst-right tree))))\n          (t\n           (cons (bst-key tree)\n                 (cons (bst-left tree)\n                       (bst-insert k (bst-right tree))))))))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing Our BST Operations\n\nLet's build a small BST and test the operations:"))

cells.append(code("; Build a BST by inserting several keys\n(defconst *bst-example*\n  (bst-insert 3\n    (bst-insert 1\n      (bst-insert 5\n        (bst-insert 2\n          (bst-insert 4 nil))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test lookup\n(list (bst-in 3 *bst-example*)   ; should be T\n      (bst-in 7 *bst-example*)   ; should be NIL\n      (bst-in 1 *bst-example*))  ; should be T"))

cells.append(code("; Verify the BST property holds\n(bst-p *bst-example*)"))

# 6. BST Theorems
cells.append(md("### Correctness Theorems for BSTs\n\nWe now prove the two most important properties of our BST implementation:\n\n1. **Preservation**: `bst-insert` preserves the BST property\n2. **Completeness**: `bst-in` finds what was inserted"))

cells.append(md("#### Theorem: Insertion Preserves BST Property\n\nThis is the key structural invariant — inserting into a valid BST yields another valid BST:"))

cells.append(code("; Helper lemmas about bst-all-< and bst-all->= under insertion\n(defthm bst-all-<-of-insert\n  (implies (and (bst-all-< tree bound)\n                (lexorder k bound)\n                (not (equal k bound)))\n           (bst-all-< (bst-insert k tree) bound)))\n\n(defthm bst-all->=-of-insert\n  (implies (and (bst-all->= tree bound)\n                (lexorder bound k))\n           (bst-all->= (bst-insert k tree) bound)))"))

cells.append(code("; Main theorem: insertion preserves the BST property\n(defthm bst-p-of-bst-insert\n  (implies (bst-p tree)\n           (bst-p (bst-insert k tree))))"))

cells.append(md("#### Theorem: Lookup Finds Inserted Keys\n\nAfter inserting key $k$, looking up $k$ returns true:"))

cells.append(code("; After inserting k, we can find k\n(defthm bst-in-of-bst-insert-same\n  (implies (bst-p tree)\n           (bst-in k (bst-insert k tree))))"))

cells.append(code("; Inserting k doesn't affect lookup of other keys\n(defthm bst-in-of-bst-insert-other\n  (implies (and (bst-p tree)\n                (not (equal j k)))\n           (equal (bst-in j (bst-insert k tree))\n                  (bst-in j tree))))"))

# 7. Balanced BSTs
cells.append(md("## 4. Balanced Binary Search Trees\n\n### Why Balance Matters\n\nAn unbalanced BST can degenerate into a linked list, making operations $O(n)$. For example, inserting keys $1, 2, 3, 4, 5$ in order produces:\n\n```\n1\n \\\n  2\n   \\\n    3\n     \\\n      4\n       \\\n        5\n```\n\nTo guarantee $O(\\log n)$ operations, we need *balanced* BSTs. Several balancing schemes exist:\n- **AVL trees**: balance factor (height difference) at most 1\n- **Red-black trees**: balanced by coloring constraints\n- **Leftist trees**: balance by rank (shortest path to nil)"))

cells.append(md("### Leftist Trees\n\nThe ACL2 community books include a verified implementation of *leftist trees* (a type of balanced heap) in `books/projects/leftist-trees/`.\n\nA leftist tree satisfies:\n1. **Heap property**: parent key $\\leq$ children keys\n2. **Leftist property**: rank of left child $\\geq$ rank of right child\n\nwhere the *rank* is the length of the rightmost path to nil.\n\nThe key insight is that the leftist property ensures the tree is \"heavy\" on the left, keeping the right spine short ($O(\\log n)$). Merge operations walk along the right spine, giving logarithmic complexity."))

cells.append(code("; Rank of a leftist tree (length of right spine)\n(defun ltree-rank (tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      0\n    (+ 1 (ltree-rank (cddr tree)))))"))

cells.append(md("### Complexity Analysis\n\nFor a balanced BST with $n$ nodes:\n\n| Operation | Balanced BST | Unbalanced BST |\n|-----------|-------------|----------------|\n| Lookup    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n| Insert    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n| Delete    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n| Min/Max   | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n\nThe theorems in the leftist tree library prove that the height is $O(\\log n)$ and that all operations preserve both the heap and leftist properties."))

# 8. Records/Structures
cells.append(md("## 5. Records and Structures\n\nFor modeling real-world data, we often want *records* with named fields. In ACL2, we can model records using alists:"))

cells.append(code("; A student record as an alist\n(defun make-student (name id gpa)\n  (list (cons 'name name)\n        (cons 'id id)\n        (cons 'gpa gpa)))\n\n(defun student-name (s) (cdr (assoc 'name s)))\n(defun student-id (s) (cdr (assoc 'id s)))\n(defun student-gpa (s) (cdr (assoc 'gpa s)))"))

cells.append(code("; Test student records\n(defconst *alice*\n  (make-student 'alice 12345 39/10))\n\n(student-name *alice*)\n(student-gpa *alice*)"))

cells.append(md("### Modeling a Stack\n\nA stack is one of the simplest data structures — it's just a list with `push` and `pop`:"))

cells.append(code("; Stack operations\n(defun stack-push (x stk) (cons x stk))\n(defun stack-pop (stk) (cdr stk))\n(defun stack-top (stk) (car stk))\n(defun stack-empty-p (stk) (endp stk))"))

cells.append(code("; Stack properties\n(defthm stack-top-of-push\n  (equal (stack-top (stack-push x stk)) x))\n\n(defthm stack-pop-of-push\n  (equal (stack-pop (stack-push x stk)) stk))"))

cells.append(md("### Modeling a Queue\n\nA queue can be modeled using two lists (front and back) for amortized $O(1)$ enqueue and dequeue:"))

cells.append(code("; Queue: (front-list . back-list)\n(defun make-queue () (cons nil nil))\n\n(defun enqueue (x q)\n  (cons (car q) (cons x (cdr q))))\n\n(defun queue-flip (q)\n  (if (consp (car q))\n      q\n    (cons (reverse (cdr q)) nil)))\n\n(defun dequeue (q)\n  (let ((q2 (queue-flip q)))\n    (cons (cdar q2) (cdr q2))))\n\n(defun queue-front (q)\n  (let ((q2 (queue-flip q)))\n    (caar q2)))\n\n(defun queue-empty-p (q)\n  (and (endp (car q)) (endp (cdr q))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test the queue\n(let* ((q (make-queue))\n       (q (enqueue 'a q))\n       (q (enqueue 'b q))\n       (q (enqueue 'c q)))\n  (list (queue-front q)\n        (queue-empty-p q)))"))

# 9. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 6. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 4.1: Alist Merge\n\nDefine a function `(alist-merge a1 a2)` that merges two alists, with keys in `a1` taking precedence over keys in `a2`. Prove that for any key `k`, `(assoc k (alist-merge a1 a2))` equals `(assoc k a1)` when present in `a1`, and `(assoc k a2)` otherwise."))

# Summary and next

cells.append(md("---\n\n**Next Module:** [Module 5: Graph Algorithms](05_graph_algorithms.ipynb) — We apply these data structure techniques to represent and reason about graphs, including DFS, BFS, and Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm."))
