import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 5: Graph Algorithms\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–4 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction, Data Structures)\n\nGraphs are one of the most versatile data structures in computer science. In this module, we represent graphs in ACL2, implement classic graph algorithms (DFS, BFS, Dijkstra), and *prove* their correctness properties. This connects the data structure foundations from Module 4 to algorithmic reasoning."))

# 2. Graph Representation
cells.append(md("## 1. Graph Representation\n\nWe represent graphs as *adjacency lists* — an alist mapping each node to its list of neighbors.\n\nFor an **unweighted** graph:\n```\n((A . (B C))     ; A connects to B and C\n (B . (A D))     ; B connects to A and D\n ...)\n```\n\nFor a **weighted** graph (used in Dijkstra's algorithm):\n```\n((A . ((B . 4) (C . 2)))    ; A→B cost 4, A→C cost 2\n (B . ((D . 3)))             ; B→D cost 3\n ...)\n```"))

cells.append(md("### Graph Predicates and Accessors\n\nWe define basic functions for working with graphs:"))

cells.append(code("; Recognizer for an unweighted graph (alist of node -> neighbor list)\n(defun graphp (g)\n  (if (endp g)\n      t\n    (and (consp (car g))\n         (true-listp (cdar g))\n         (graphp (cdr g)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Get the neighbors of a node in graph g\n(defun neighbors (node g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal node g)))\n\n; Get all nodes in the graph\n(defun nodes (g)\n  (strip-cars g))"))

cells.append(code("; Is there an edge from u to v?\n(defun edge-p (u v g)\n  (member-equal v (neighbors u g)))"))

cells.append(md("### Example Graph\n\nLet's define a sample undirected graph:\n\n```\n    A --- B\n    |     |\n    C --- D --- E\n```"))

cells.append(code("; An undirected graph represented as an adjacency list\n; Each edge appears in both directions\n(defconst *example-graph*\n  '((a . (b c))\n    (b . (a d))\n    (c . (a d))\n    (d . (b c e))\n    (e . (d))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test the graph operations\n(list (graphp *example-graph*)\n      (neighbors 'd *example-graph*)\n      (nodes *example-graph*)\n      (edge-p 'a 'b *example-graph*)\n      (edge-p 'a 'e *example-graph*))"))

cells.append(md("### Undirectedness Property\n\nAn undirected graph requires that if $(u, v)$ is an edge, then $(v, u)$ is also an edge:"))

cells.append(code("; Check if a graph is undirected\n(defun undirected-p (g nodes)\n  (if (endp nodes)\n      t\n    (let* ((u (car nodes))\n           (nbrs (neighbors u g)))\n      (and (undirected-helper u nbrs g)\n           (undirected-p g (cdr nodes))))))\n\n(defun undirected-helper (u nbrs g)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      t\n    (and (member-equal u (neighbors (car nbrs) g))\n         (undirected-helper u (cdr nbrs) g))))"))

# 3. DFS
cells.append(md("## 2. Graph Traversal: Depth-First Search (DFS)\n\nDFS explores a graph by going as deep as possible along each branch before backtracking. We use the *accumulator pattern* to track visited nodes.\n\n### Key Idea\n- Maintain a `visited` list to avoid revisiting nodes\n- For each unvisited neighbor, recursively visit it\n- The accumulator grows monotonically (we only add to it, never remove)"))

cells.append(code("; Depth-First Search\n; Returns the list of all nodes reachable from 'start'\n(defun dfs-visit (node g visited)\n  (if (member-equal node visited)\n      visited\n    (dfs-visit-neighbors (neighbors node g)\n                         g\n                         (cons node visited))))\n\n(defun dfs-visit-neighbors (nbrs g visited)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      visited\n    (dfs-visit-neighbors (cdr nbrs)\n                         g\n                         (dfs-visit (car nbrs) g visited))))"))

cells.append(code("; Run DFS starting from node A\n(dfs-visit 'a *example-graph* nil)"))

cells.append(md("### DFS Properties\n\nWe can state (and ideally prove) key properties of DFS:\n1. Every node in the result was reachable from the start\n2. Every reachable node appears in the result\n3. The visited list only grows"))

cells.append(code("; Property: visited list only grows (monotonicity)\n(defthm dfs-visit-monotonic\n  (subsetp-equal visited\n                 (dfs-visit node g visited)))"))

cells.append(code("; Property: the start node is in the result\n(defthm start-in-dfs\n  (member-equal node\n                (dfs-visit node g visited)))"))

# 4. BFS
cells.append(md("## 3. Breadth-First Search (BFS)\n\nBFS explores a graph level by level, visiting all neighbors of the current node before moving deeper. We use a *queue* (represented as a list) to track nodes to visit.\n\n### BFS vs DFS\n| Property | DFS | BFS |\n|----------|-----|-----|\n| Data structure | Stack (call stack) | Queue |\n| Order | Deep first | Broad first |\n| Shortest path? | No | Yes (unweighted) |\n| Space | $O(\\text{depth})$ | $O(\\text{width})$ |"))

cells.append(code("; Breadth-First Search\n; queue: list of nodes to visit\n; visited: nodes already visited\n(defun bfs-step (queue g visited)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (let ((unseen (set-difference-equal (nodes g) visited)))\n                             (+ (len unseen) (len queue)))))\n  (if (endp queue)\n      visited\n    (let ((node (car queue))\n          (rest (cdr queue)))\n      (if (member-equal node visited)\n          (bfs-step rest g visited)\n        (let ((new-nbrs (set-difference-equal\n                          (neighbors node g)\n                          visited)))\n          (bfs-step (append rest new-nbrs)\n                    g\n                    (cons node visited)))))))"))

cells.append(code("; BFS entry point\n(defun bfs (start g)\n  (bfs-step (list start) g nil))"))

cells.append(code("; Run BFS from node A\n(bfs 'a *example-graph*)"))

cells.append(md("### BFS Gives Shortest Paths\n\nIn an unweighted graph, BFS visits nodes in order of their distance from the start. The first time BFS reaches a node, it has found the shortest path (fewest edges). This is why BFS is preferred over DFS for shortest-path queries in unweighted graphs."))

# 5. Dijkstra
cells.append(md("## 4. Shortest Paths: Dijkstra's Algorithm\n\nFor *weighted* graphs (where edges have costs), we need Dijkstra's algorithm to find shortest paths. The ACL2 community books contain a fully verified implementation in `books/misc/dijkstra-shortest-path.lisp` with 126 theorems.\n\nWe present a simplified version here to illustrate the core ideas.\n\n### Key Concepts\n\n- **Distance table**: an alist mapping each node to its current best known distance\n- **Relaxation**: when we find a shorter path to a node, we update its distance\n- **Priority queue**: we always process the unvisited node with the smallest distance"))

cells.append(md("### Weighted Graph Representation\n\nFor Dijkstra, we use a graph where each neighbor is paired with an edge weight:"))

cells.append(code("; Weighted graph: alist of (node . ((neighbor1 . weight1) (neighbor2 . weight2) ...))\n(defconst *weighted-graph*\n  '((a . ((b . 4) (c . 2)))\n    (b . ((d . 3) (e . 1)))\n    (c . ((b . 1) (d . 5)))\n    (d . ((e . 2)))\n    (e . ())))"))

cells.append(code("; Get weighted neighbors: list of (neighbor . weight) pairs\n(defun weighted-neighbors (node g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal node g)))\n\n; Get the weight of edge from u to v (nil if no edge)\n(defun edge-weight (u v g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal v (weighted-neighbors u g))))"))

cells.append(md("### Distance Table Operations\n\nThe distance table maps nodes to their current best-known distance from the source:"))

cells.append(code("; Distance table: alist of (node . distance)\n; 'inf represents infinity (unreachable)\n\n(defun get-dist (node dist-table)\n  (let ((pair (assoc-equal node dist-table)))\n    (if pair (cdr pair) 'inf)))\n\n(defun set-dist (node d dist-table)\n  (acons node d dist-table))\n\n; Is distance a < distance b? (handling infinity)\n(defun dist< (a b)\n  (cond ((equal b 'inf) (not (equal a 'inf)))\n        ((equal a 'inf) nil)\n        (t (< a b))))"))

cells.append(md("### Relaxation\n\nThe *relaxation step* checks whether going through node `u` gives a shorter path to neighbor `v`:"))

cells.append(code("; Relax a single edge: if going through u to v is shorter, update\n(defun relax-edge (u v w dist-table)\n  (let ((du (get-dist u dist-table))\n        (dv (get-dist v dist-table)))\n    (if (and (not (equal du 'inf))\n             (dist< (+ du w) dv))\n        (set-dist v (+ du w) dist-table)\n      dist-table)))\n\n; Relax all neighbors of node u\n(defun relax-neighbors (u nbrs dist-table)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      dist-table\n    (let* ((v (caar nbrs))\n           (w (cdar nbrs))\n           (new-dist (relax-edge u v w dist-table)))\n      (relax-neighbors u (cdr nbrs) new-dist))))"))

cells.append(md("### Dijkstra's Algorithm\n\nThe main loop: repeatedly pick the unvisited node with smallest distance, relax its neighbors, and mark it visited:"))

cells.append(code("; Find the unvisited node with the smallest distance\n(defun find-min-node (nodes dist-table visited)\n  (if (endp nodes)\n      nil\n    (let ((node (car nodes)))\n      (if (member-equal node visited)\n          (find-min-node (cdr nodes) dist-table visited)\n        (let ((rest-min (find-min-node (cdr nodes) dist-table visited)))\n          (if (null rest-min)\n              node\n            (if (dist< (get-dist node dist-table)\n                       (get-dist rest-min dist-table))\n                node\n              rest-min)))))))"))

cells.append(code("; One step of Dijkstra: pick min node, relax neighbors\n(defun dijkstra-step (g all-nodes dist-table visited)\n  (let ((u (find-min-node all-nodes dist-table visited)))\n    (if (null u)\n        (mv dist-table visited)\n      (let* ((nbrs (weighted-neighbors u g))\n             (new-dist (relax-neighbors u nbrs dist-table))\n             (new-visited (cons u visited)))\n        (dijkstra-step g all-nodes new-dist new-visited)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Dijkstra's algorithm: find shortest distances from source\n(defun dijkstra (source g)\n  (let* ((all-nodes (strip-cars g))\n         (dist-table (set-dist source 0 nil)))\n    (dijkstra-step g all-nodes dist-table nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Run Dijkstra from node A\n(dijkstra 'a *weighted-graph*)"))

cells.append(md("### Correctness of Dijkstra's Algorithm\n\nThe full correctness proof (in `books/misc/dijkstra-shortest-path.lisp`) establishes:\n\n1. **Optimality**: The distance returned for each node is the true shortest-path distance\n2. **Triangle inequality**: For any edge $(u, v, w)$: $d(v) \\leq d(u) + w$\n3. **Termination**: The algorithm terminates because the visited set grows at each step\n\nThese 126 theorems in the ACL2 community books form one of the most thorough verified implementations of Dijkstra's algorithm."))

# 6. Path Finding
cells.append(md("## 5. Path Finding\n\nBeyond computing distances, we often need to find an actual *path* between two nodes. We define a path predicate and a path-finding function."))

cells.append(code("; A path is a list of nodes\n; pathp checks: is this a valid path in graph g?\n(defun pathp (path g)\n  (cond ((endp path) t)\n        ((endp (cdr path)) (member-equal (car path) (nodes g)))\n        (t (and (edge-p (car path) (cadr path) g)\n                (pathp (cdr path) g)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Find a path from start to end using DFS\n; Returns a path (list of nodes) or nil if no path exists\n(defun find-path (start end g visited)\n  (cond ((equal start end) (list start))\n        ((member-equal start visited) nil)\n        (t (find-path-neighbors\n             (neighbors start g)\n             start end g\n             (cons start visited)))))\n\n(defun find-path-neighbors (nbrs start end g visited)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      nil\n    (let ((sub-path (find-path (car nbrs) end g visited)))\n      (if sub-path\n          (cons start sub-path)\n        (find-path-neighbors (cdr nbrs) start end g visited)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Find a path from A to E\n(find-path 'a 'e *example-graph* nil)"))

cells.append(code("; Verify the path is valid\n(pathp (find-path 'a 'e *example-graph* nil)\n       *example-graph*)"))

cells.append(md("### Path Correctness\n\nWe can state the key property: if `find-path` returns a non-nil result, it is a valid path:"))

cells.append(code("; The path returned by find-path (if non-nil) is a valid path\n(defthm find-path-is-valid\n  (implies (find-path start end g visited)\n           (pathp (find-path start end g visited) g)))"))

# 7. Cycle Detection
cells.append(md("## 6. Cycle Detection\n\nA *cycle* is a path that starts and ends at the same node. Detecting cycles is crucial for many algorithms (topological sort, dependency analysis, etc.).\n\n### White-Gray-Black Coloring\n\nThe classic DFS-based cycle detection uses three colors:\n- **White**: not yet visited\n- **Gray**: currently being explored (on the DFS stack)\n- **Black**: fully explored\n\nA *back edge* (an edge to a gray node) indicates a cycle in a directed graph."))

cells.append(code("; Get the color of a node from the color map\n(defun get-color (node colors)\n  (let ((pair (assoc-equal node colors)))\n    (if pair (cdr pair) 'white)))\n\n(defun set-color (node color colors)\n  (acons node color colors))"))

cells.append(code("; DFS-based cycle detection for directed graphs\n; Returns (mv has-cycle colors)\n(defun detect-cycle-visit (node g colors)\n  (let ((colors (set-color node 'gray colors)))\n    (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n      (detect-cycle-neighbors (neighbors node g) g colors)\n      (if cycle-found\n          (mv t colors)\n        (mv nil (set-color node 'black colors))))))\n\n(defun detect-cycle-neighbors (nbrs g colors)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      (mv nil colors)\n    (let ((v (car nbrs))\n          (c (get-color (car nbrs) colors)))\n      (cond\n        ((equal c 'gray)\n         (mv t colors))  ; back edge found = cycle!\n        ((equal c 'white)\n         (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n           (detect-cycle-visit v g colors)\n           (if cycle-found\n               (mv t colors)\n             (detect-cycle-neighbors (cdr nbrs) g colors))))\n        (t ; black - already fully explored\n         (detect-cycle-neighbors (cdr nbrs) g colors))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Check all nodes for cycles\n(defun has-cycle (g)\n  (has-cycle-nodes (nodes g) g nil))\n\n(defun has-cycle-nodes (node-list g colors)\n  (if (endp node-list)\n      nil\n    (if (equal (get-color (car node-list) colors) 'white)\n        (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n          (detect-cycle-visit (car node-list) g colors)\n          (if cycle-found\n              t\n            (has-cycle-nodes (cdr node-list) g colors)))\n      (has-cycle-nodes (cdr node-list) g colors))))"))

cells.append(code("; A directed graph with a cycle: A -> B -> C -> A\n(defconst *cyclic-graph*\n  '((a . (b))\n    (b . (c))\n    (c . (a))))\n\n; A directed acyclic graph (DAG)\n(defconst *dag*\n  '((a . (b c))\n    (b . (d))\n    (c . (d))\n    (d . ())))\n\n(list (has-cycle *cyclic-graph*)   ; should be T\n      (has-cycle *dag*))           ; should be NIL"))

# 8. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 7. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 5.1: Connected Components\n\nDefine a function `(connected-components g)` that returns a list of lists, where each inner list is the set of nodes in one connected component of an undirected graph.\n\n*Hint:* Use DFS from each unvisited node to collect one component."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 5.1: Connected components\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

Every node appears before all nodes it has edges to.\n\n*Hint:* Use DFS and add each node to the result *after* visiting all its neighbors (reverse postorder)."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 5.2: Topological sort\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

Then define `(reconstruct-path pred-table source dest)` that returns the actual shortest path.\n\nProve that the reconstructed path is a valid path in the graph."))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 6: Machine Models — The M1 Stack Machine\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–5 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction, Data Structures, Graph Algorithms)\n\nIn this module, we study how to *model a computer* inside a theorem prover and *prove that programs are correct*. We use the M1 machine — a simplified JVM-like stack machine designed by J Strother Moore for teaching program verification with ACL2. The M1 machine is defined in `books/demos/marktoberdorf-08/m1.lisp`."))

# 2. Why Machine Models?
cells.append(md("## 1. Why Machine Models?\n\nSoftware verification ultimately requires a *formal model of computation*. Without one, we can prove properties of mathematical functions (as we did in Modules 2–3), but we cannot prove that an *actual program* computes the right answer.\n\nA machine model gives us:\n- A precise definition of what each instruction does\n- A state that we can reason about formally\n- A `step` function that describes one execution step\n- A `run` function that executes multiple steps\n\nOnce we have a machine model, we can:\n1. Write programs as instruction sequences\n2. Run programs symbolically\n3. Prove that a program computes the correct mathematical function\n4. Verify loop invariants using induction"))

cells.append(md("### The Verification Pipeline\n\n$$\\text{Specification (math function)} \\xleftarrow{\\text{prove } =} \\text{Program on Machine Model} \\xrightarrow{\\text{compile}} \\text{Real Hardware}$$\n\nIf we prove that the program on the model computes the specification, and we trust that the model faithfully represents the real machine, then we have end-to-end correctness."))

# 3. The M1 Machine
cells.append(md("## 2. The M1 Machine\n\nM1 is a tiny JVM-like (Java Virtual Machine) stack machine with:\n- A **program counter** (pc): index into the instruction list\n- **Local variables**: a list of values, accessed by index\n- An **operand stack**: used for arithmetic and comparisons\n- A **program**: a list of instructions\n\nM1 supports integer arithmetic, conditional branching, and loops — enough to implement and verify interesting algorithms."))

cells.append(md("### M1 Instruction Set\n\n| Instruction | Effect | Stack |\n|-------------|--------|-------|\n| `(ICONST k)` | Push constant $k$ onto stack | $\\ldots \\Rightarrow \\ldots, k$ |\n| `(ILOAD i)` | Push `locals[i]` onto stack | $\\ldots \\Rightarrow \\ldots, v$ |\n| `(ISTORE i)` | Pop top into `locals[i]` | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IADD)` | Pop two, push their sum | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a+b$ |\n| `(ISUB)` | Pop two, push difference | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a-b$ |\n| `(IMUL)` | Pop two, push product | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a \\times b$ |\n| `(IFEQ offset)` | Pop; if 0, jump by offset | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IFLE offset)` | Pop; if $\\leq 0$, jump by offset | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IFLT offset)` | Pop; if $< 0$, jump by offset | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(GOTO offset)` | Unconditional jump by offset | (unchanged) |\n| `(HALT)` | Stop execution | (unchanged) |"))

# 4. Defining the Machine
cells.append(md("## 3. Defining the Machine\n\n### State Representation\n\nThe M1 state is a 4-tuple: `(pc locals stack program)`."))

cells.append(code("; M1 State constructor and accessors\n(defun make-state (pc locals stack program)\n  (list pc locals stack program))\n\n(defun pc (s) (nth 0 s))\n(defun locals (s) (nth 1 s))\n(defun stack (s) (nth 2 s))\n(defun program (s) (nth 3 s))"))

cells.append(md("### Stack Operations\n\nThe operand stack uses standard push/pop:"))

cells.append(code("; Stack operations for M1\n(defun push-stack (v stk) (cons v stk))\n(defun top-stack (stk) (car stk))\n(defun pop-stack (stk) (cdr stk))"))

cells.append(md("### Instruction Fetch\n\nTo fetch the current instruction, we index into the program using the pc:"))

cells.append(code("; Fetch the current instruction\n(defun current-instruction (s)\n  (nth (pc s) (program s)))"))

cells.append(md("### Instruction Operands\n\nSome instructions carry an operand (e.g., `ICONST`, `ILOAD`, `GOTO`):"))

cells.append(code("; Extract the opcode and operand from an instruction\n(defun opcode (inst) (car inst))\n(defun arg1 (inst) (cadr inst))"))

cells.append(md("### Local Variable Access\n\nTo read and write local variables:"))

cells.append(code("; Read local variable at index i\n(defun local-var (i locals)\n  (nth i locals))\n\n; Write value v to local variable at index i\n(defun update-local (i v locals)\n  (update-nth i v locals))"))

cells.append(md("### The Step Function\n\nThe heart of M1: a function that executes one instruction and returns the new state:"))

cells.append(code("; Execute one instruction\n(defun execute-instruction (inst s)\n  (let ((op (opcode inst)))\n    (case op\n      (ICONST\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (arg1 inst) (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ILOAD\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (local-var (arg1 inst) (locals s))\n                               (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ISTORE\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (update-local (arg1 inst)\n                                 (top-stack (stack s))\n                                 (locals s))\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IADD\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (+ (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ISUB\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (- (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IMUL\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (* (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFEQ\n       (make-state (if (equal (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFLE\n       (make-state (if (<= (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFLT\n       (make-state (if (< (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (GOTO\n       (make-state (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (stack s)\n                   (program s)))\n      (HALT s)\n      (otherwise s))))"))

cells.append(md("### The Step and Run Functions\n\nWe define `step` (one step) and `run` (multiple steps using a clock):"))

cells.append(code("; Take one step (if not halted)\n(defun m1-step (s)\n  (let ((inst (current-instruction s)))\n    (if (equal (opcode inst) 'HALT)\n        s\n      (execute-instruction inst s))))"))

cells.append(code("; Run for n steps (the 'clock')\n(defun run (s n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      s\n    (run (m1-step s) (- n 1))))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing the Machine\n\nLet's run a simple program that computes $3 + 4$:"))

cells.append(code("; Program: push 3, push 4, add, halt\n; Expected result: 7 on top of stack\n(defconst *add-program*\n  '((ICONST 3)\n    (ICONST 4)\n    (IADD)\n    (HALT)))\n\n(run (make-state 0 nil nil *add-program*) 3)"))

cells.append(code("; Check the result: top of stack should be 7\n(top-stack\n  (stack\n    (run (make-state 0 nil nil *add-program*) 3)))"))

# 5. Factorial Example
cells.append(md("## 4. Example: Factorial Program\n\nNow let's tackle a real program: computing $n!$ (factorial).\n\n### Mathematical Specification\n\nFirst, we define the mathematical factorial function:"))

cells.append(code("; Mathematical factorial\n(defun fact (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      1\n    (* n (fact (- n 1)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Quick test\n(list (fact 0) (fact 1) (fact 5) (fact 10))"))

cells.append(md("### The Factorial Program in M1 Bytecode\n\nHere is $n!$ as an M1 program. The local variables are:\n- `locals[0]` = n (input, counts down)\n- `locals[1]` = accumulator (result, starts at 1)\n\nThe algorithm: `acc = 1; while (n > 0) { acc = acc * n; n = n - 1; }`\n\n```\npc  Instruction       Comment\n--  -----------       -------\n 0  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 1  (IFEQ 10)         if n == 0, jump to HALT (pc 11)\n 2  (ILOAD 1)         push acc\n 3  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 4  (IMUL)            acc * n\n 5  (ISTORE 1)        store into acc\n 6  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 7  (ICONST 1)        push 1\n 8  (ISUB)            n - 1\n 9  (ISTORE 0)        store into n\n10  (GOTO -10)        jump back to pc 0\n11  (ILOAD 1)         push acc (the result)\n12  (HALT)\n```"))

cells.append(code("; Factorial program for M1\n(defconst *factorial-program*\n  '((ILOAD 0)          ; 0: push n\n    (IFEQ 10)          ; 1: if n=0 goto 11\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 2: push acc\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 3: push n\n    (IMUL)             ; 4: acc*n\n    (ISTORE 1)         ; 5: acc = acc*n\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 6: push n\n    (ICONST 1)         ; 7: push 1\n    (ISUB)             ; 8: n-1\n    (ISTORE 0)         ; 9: n = n-1\n    (GOTO -10)         ; 10: goto 0\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 11: push result\n    (HALT)))           ; 12: stop"))

cells.append(md("### Running the Factorial Program\n\nLet's compute $5! = 120$:"))

cells.append(code("; Run factorial(5)\n; Initial state: pc=0, locals=(5 1), stack=nil\n(defconst *fact-5-result*\n  (run (make-state 0 '(5 1) nil *factorial-program*) 100))\n\n*fact-5-result*"))

cells.append(code("; The answer should be on top of the stack\n(top-stack (stack *fact-5-result*))"))

cells.append(code("; Verify: does it match the mathematical factorial?\n(equal (top-stack (stack *fact-5-result*))\n       (fact 5))"))

cells.append(md("### Step-by-Step Execution\n\nLet's trace the first few steps to understand how the machine works:"))

cells.append(code("; State after each step for factorial(3)\n(let ((s0 (make-state 0 '(3 1) nil *factorial-program*)))\n  (list\n    (list 'step-0 s0)\n    (list 'step-1 (run s0 1))    ; ILOAD 0: push 3\n    (list 'step-2 (run s0 2))    ; IFEQ: 3≠0, continue\n    (list 'step-3 (run s0 3))    ; ILOAD 1: push acc=1\n    (list 'step-4 (run s0 4))    ; ILOAD 0: push n=3\n    (list 'step-5 (run s0 5))    ; IMUL: 1*3=3\n    (list 'step-6 (run s0 6))))  ; ISTORE 1: acc=3"))

# 6. Iterative Sum Example
cells.append(md("## 5. Example: Iterative Sum\n\nLet's verify a program that computes $\\sum_{i=1}^{n} i = \\frac{n(n+1)}{2}$.\n\n### Mathematical Specification"))

cells.append(code("; Mathematical sum from 1 to n\n(defun sum-1-to-n (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      0\n    (+ n (sum-1-to-n (- n 1)))))"))

cells.append(code("; The closed form: n*(n+1)/2\n(defthm sum-closed-form\n  (implies (natp n)\n           (equal (sum-1-to-n n)\n                  (/ (* n (+ n 1)) 2))))"))

cells.append(md("### The Sum Program\n\nLocal variables:\n- `locals[0]` = n (counts down)\n- `locals[1]` = sum (accumulator, starts at 0)\n\nAlgorithm: `sum = 0; while (n > 0) { sum = sum + n; n = n - 1; }`"))

cells.append(code("; Sum program for M1\n(defconst *sum-program*\n  '((ILOAD 0)          ; 0: push n\n    (IFEQ 10)          ; 1: if n=0 goto 11\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 2: push sum\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 3: push n\n    (IADD)             ; 4: sum + n\n    (ISTORE 1)         ; 5: sum = sum + n\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 6: push n\n    (ICONST 1)         ; 7: push 1\n    (ISUB)             ; 8: n - 1\n    (ISTORE 0)         ; 9: n = n - 1\n    (GOTO -10)         ; 10: goto 0\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 11: push result\n    (HALT)))           ; 12: stop"))

cells.append(code("; Run sum(10)\n(let ((result (run (make-state 0 '(10 0) nil *sum-program*) 200)))\n  (list (top-stack (stack result))\n        (sum-1-to-n 10)\n        (/ (* 10 11) 2)))"))

# 7. Clock Functions
cells.append(md("## 6. Clock Functions\n\nThe key proof technique for M1 programs is the *clock function*. A clock function tells us exactly how many steps a program needs to reach completion.\n\n### Why Clocks?\n\nTo prove `(run state n)` produces the right answer, we need:\n1. The right number of steps `n` (the *clock*)\n2. A proof that after `n` steps, the result is correct\n\nThe clock function maps the input to the number of steps needed:"))

cells.append(code("; Clock function for the factorial program\n; Each loop iteration takes 11 steps, final exit takes 2\n(defun fact-clock (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      2  ; test n, jump to load result, halt\n    (+ 11 (fact-clock (- n 1)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: how many steps for factorial(5)?\n(fact-clock 5)"))

cells.append(code("; The M1 factorial function: run with the right clock\n(defun m1-factorial (n)\n  (top-stack\n    (stack\n      (run (make-state 0 (list n 1) nil *factorial-program*)\n           (fact-clock n)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test that m1-factorial matches fact\n(list (equal (m1-factorial 0) (fact 0))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 1) (fact 1))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 5) (fact 5))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 10) (fact 10)))"))

cells.append(md("### The Correctness Theorem\n\nThe main theorem we want to prove:\n\n$$\\forall n \\in \\mathbb{N}: \\text{m1-factorial}(n) = n!$$\n\nThis says: running the factorial program on M1 for `(fact-clock n)` steps produces the mathematical factorial."))

cells.append(code("; The key theorem: M1 factorial equals mathematical factorial\n; This requires a loop invariant (see next section)\n(defthm m1-factorial-is-fact\n  (implies (natp n)\n           (equal (m1-factorial n)\n                  (fact n))))"))

# 8. Loop Invariants
cells.append(md("## 7. Loop Invariants in M1\n\n### The Classical Approach\n\nProving a loop correct requires a *loop invariant* — a property that:\n1. Holds when the loop starts (*initialization*)\n2. Is preserved by each iteration (*maintenance*)\n3. Combined with the exit condition, implies the postcondition (*termination*)\n\nThis is exactly the Hoare logic approach to verification:\n\n$$\\{\\text{Inv} \\land \\text{condition}\\} \\;\\text{loop body}\\; \\{\\text{Inv}\\}$$"))

cells.append(md("### Loop Invariant for Factorial\n\nFor the factorial program, the loop invariant at pc=0 is:\n\n$$\\text{acc} \\times n! = N!$$\n\nwhere $N$ is the *original* input and $n$ is the *current* value of the counter.\n\n- **Initialization**: $\\text{acc} = 1$ and $n = N$, so $1 \\times N! = N!$ ✓\n- **Maintenance**: After one iteration, $\\text{acc}' = \\text{acc} \\times n$ and $n' = n - 1$, so $\\text{acc}' \\times n'! = (\\text{acc} \\times n) \\times (n-1)! = \\text{acc} \\times n! = N!$ ✓\n- **Termination**: When $n = 0$, $\\text{acc} \\times 0! = \\text{acc} \\times 1 = \\text{acc} = N!$ ✓"))

cells.append(code("; Loop invariant: acc * fact(n) = fact(N)\n; We encode this as a theorem about partial runs\n(defun fact-loop-invariant (n acc)\n  (* acc (fact n)))"))

cells.append(code("; The invariant is preserved by one loop iteration\n(defthm fact-invariant-maintained\n  (implies (and (natp n)\n                (not (zp n)))\n           (equal (fact-loop-invariant (- n 1) (* acc n))\n                  (fact-loop-invariant n acc))))"))

cells.append(code("; At termination (n=0), the invariant gives us the answer\n(defthm fact-invariant-at-exit\n  (equal (fact-loop-invariant 0 acc)\n         acc))"))

cells.append(md("### Connection to Hoare Logic\n\nThe M1 verification approach is closely related to *Hoare logic*, the classical framework for program verification:\n\n| Hoare Logic | M1 Approach |\n|---|---|\n| Precondition $\\{P\\}$ | Initial state conditions |\n| Postcondition $\\{Q\\}$ | Properties of final state |\n| Loop invariant | Invariant at loop head (pc=0) |\n| Weakest precondition | Clock function derivation |\n| Proof rule for loops | Induction on clock |\n\nThe advantage of the M1 approach is that everything is mechanically checked by ACL2 — there are no gaps in the reasoning."))

cells.append(md("### Verification Methodology Summary\n\nTo verify an M1 program:\n\n1. **Write the program** as M1 bytecode\n2. **Define the specification** as a mathematical function\n3. **Define the clock function** computing the number of steps needed\n4. **State the loop invariant** relating machine state to the specification\n5. **Prove the invariant is maintained** through each loop iteration\n6. **Prove the final theorem**: running with the clock gives the specification\n\nThis methodology scales to any M1 program and can be extended to more realistic machine models."))

# 9. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 8. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 6.1: Multiplication by Repeated Addition\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes $a \\times b$ using repeated addition (no IMUL instruction). Define the clock function and prove that your program computes `(* a b)`.\n\n*Hint:* Use locals[0]=a, locals[1]=b (counts down), locals[2]=result (accumulator)."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.1: Multiplication by repeated addition\n; Define the program, clock function, and correctness theorem\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 6.2: Fibonacci\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes the $n$-th Fibonacci number. Use locals[0]=n (counter), locals[1]=fib(k), locals[2]=fib(k-1).\n\nDefine the mathematical Fibonacci function and prove your M1 program matches it."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.2: Fibonacci on M1\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 6.3: Exponentiation\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes $b^n$ (base $b$ to the power $n$) using repeated multiplication. Define the clock function and prove correctness.\n\n*Bonus:* Can you write a version using fast exponentiation (squaring)? What does the clock function look like?"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.3: Exponentiation on M1\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

Define its mathematical specification\n3. Define the clock function\n4. State and prove the loop invariant\n5. Prove the correctness theorem"))

# Summary
cells.append(md("## Summary\n\nIn this module, we learned:\n\n- **Machine models** provide a formal foundation for program verification\n- **M1** is a simple JVM-like stack machine with 11 instructions\n- The **state** consists of `(pc, locals, stack, program)`\n- **Clock functions** determine how many steps a program needs\n- **Loop invariants** are the key technique for verifying loops\n- The methodology connects directly to **Hoare logic**\n\n### The Big Picture\n\n| Module | What We Verified |\n|--------|------------------|\n| 2–3 | Mathematical functions (factorial, append, etc.) |\n| 4 | Data structure operations (BST insert, lookup) |\n| 5 | Graph algorithms (DFS, Dijkstra) |\n| **6** | **Programs running on a machine model** |\n\nThis progression mirrors the real hierarchy of verification:\n$$\\text{Math} \\rightarrow \\text{Data Structures} \\rightarrow \\text{Algorithms} \\rightarrow \\text{Programs}$$\n\nThe M1 machine demonstrates that formal verification is not limited to abstract mathematics — it can verify *actual code* executing on *actual machines*."))

You now have the foundations to explore more advanced topics:\n\n- **Compiler verification**: Proving that a compiler correctly translates source programs to M1 bytecode\n- **Operating system verification**: Modeling process scheduling and memory management\n- **Hardware verification**: Using ACL2 to verify processor designs (as done at AMD and other companies)\n- **Cryptographic verification**: Proving properties of cryptographic algorithms\n\nThe ACL2 community books contain thousands of verified programs, algorithms, and machine models to learn from."))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 4: Data Structures — Binary Search Trees\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–3 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction)\n\nIn this module, we explore how to represent and reason about data structures in ACL2. Unlike object-oriented languages, ACL2 encapsulates data using *predicates* (recognizers) rather than classes. We will build up from simple association lists to binary search trees, proving key correctness properties along the way."))

# 2. Data structures philosophy
cells.append(md("## 1. Introduction to Data Structures in ACL2\n\nIn languages like Java or Python, data structures are defined using classes with fields and methods. In ACL2, we take a different approach:\n\n- **Data** is represented using lists, cons pairs, and atoms\n- **Structure** is enforced by *predicates* (recognizer functions) that check whether a value has the right shape\n- **Operations** are ordinary functions that assume their inputs satisfy the recognizer\n- **Correctness** is established by *theorems* relating operations to the recognizer\n\nEvery data structure in ACL2 follows this pattern:\n\n1. **Representation**: Choose how to represent the data using cons pairs, lists, etc.\n2. **Recognizer**: Define a predicate `foo-p` that checks if a value is a valid instance\n3. **Accessors**: Define functions to extract components\n4. **Operations**: Define functions that manipulate the structure\n5. **Theorems**: Prove that operations preserve the recognizer and satisfy key properties"))

# 3. Association Lists
cells.append(md("## 2. Association Lists (Alists)\n\nThe simplest key-value data structure in ACL2 is the *association list* (alist). An alist is a list of key-value pairs, where each pair is a cons cell `(key . value)`.\n\n| Function | Description |\n|----------|-------------|\n| `(acons key val alist)` | Add a key-value pair to the front |\n| `(assoc key alist)` | Look up a key (returns the pair or nil) |\n| `(alistp x)` | Recognizer for alists |\n| `(strip-cars alist)` | Extract all keys |\n| `(strip-cdrs alist)` | Extract all values |"))

cells.append(code("; Build a phone book as an alist\n(defconst *phone-book*\n  (acons 'alice 5551234\n    (acons 'bob 5555678\n      (acons 'carol 5559999 nil))))\n\n; Look up an entry\n(assoc 'bob *phone-book*)"))

cells.append(code("; Verify it's a valid alist and inspect keys/values\n(list (alistp *phone-book*)\n      (strip-cars *phone-book*)\n      (strip-cdrs *phone-book*))"))

cells.append(md("### Properties of Alists\n\nA fundamental property: looking up a key you just inserted always succeeds:"))

cells.append(code("; Key property: assoc finds what acons just added\n(thm\n  (equal (assoc k (acons k v alist))\n         (cons k v)))"))

cells.append(code("; If we look up a different key, acons doesn't interfere\n(thm\n  (implies (not (equal j k))\n           (equal (assoc j (acons k v alist))\n                  (assoc j alist))))"))

cells.append(md("### Defining Our Own Alist Operations"))

cells.append(code("; Remove all pairs with a given key\n(defun alist-remove (key alist)\n  (cond ((endp alist) nil)\n        ((equal key (caar alist))\n         (alist-remove key (cdr alist)))\n        (t (cons (car alist)\n                 (alist-remove key (cdr alist))))))\n\n; After removing key k, looking up a different key j is unaffected\n(defthm assoc-of-alist-remove\n  (implies (not (equal j k))\n           (equal (assoc j (alist-remove k alist))\n                  (assoc j alist))))"))

cells.append(code("; Update: remove old binding, then add new one\n(defun alist-update (key val alist)\n  (acons key val (alist-remove key alist)))"))

# 4. Binary Search Trees intro
cells.append(md("## 3. Binary Search Trees\n\nAssociation lists are simple but inefficient: lookup is $O(n)$. For better performance, we use *binary search trees* (BSTs), which provide $O(\\log n)$ lookup when balanced.\n\n### Representation\n\nWe represent BSTs using nested cons cells:\n- `nil` represents an empty BST\n- A non-empty BST node is `(key left . right)` where `left` and `right` are BSTs\n\nThis is a natural Lisp representation that avoids the overhead of more complex structures."))

cells.append(code("; A BST node is (key left . right) where left and right are BSTs\n; nil represents an empty BST\n(defun bst-key (node) (car node))\n(defun bst-left (node) (cadr node))\n(defun bst-right (node) (cddr node))"))

cells.append(code("; Recognizer for a BST node (non-nil node with a key)\n(defun bst-node-p (x)\n  (and (consp x)\n       (consp (cdr x))))"))

cells.append(md("### The BST Ordering Property\n\nA binary search tree must satisfy the *BST property*: for every node with key $k$,\n- All keys in the left subtree are $< k$\n- All keys in the right subtree are $\\geq k$\n\nWe use `lexorder` for comparisons and helper predicates for bounding:"))

cells.append(code("; All keys in tree are less than bound\n(defun bst-all-< (tree bound)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (lexorder (bst-key tree) bound)\n         (not (equal (bst-key tree) bound))\n         (bst-all-< (bst-left tree) bound)\n         (bst-all-< (bst-right tree) bound))))\n\n; All keys in tree are greater than or equal to bound\n(defun bst-all->= (tree bound)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (lexorder bound (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-all->= (bst-left tree) bound)\n         (bst-all->= (bst-right tree) bound))))"))

cells.append(code("; The BST property: ordered binary tree\n(defun bst-p (tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      t\n    (and (bst-node-p tree)\n         (bst-all-< (bst-left tree) (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-all->= (bst-right tree) (bst-key tree))\n         (bst-p (bst-left tree))\n         (bst-p (bst-right tree)))))"))

# 5. BST Operations
cells.append(md("### BST Lookup and Insertion\n\nTo search for a key, compare with the current node and recurse into the appropriate subtree. To insert, walk down to the correct position and create a new leaf:"))

cells.append(code("; Lookup: is key k in the BST?\n(defun bst-in (k tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      nil\n    (cond ((equal k (bst-key tree)) t)\n          ((lexorder k (bst-key tree))\n           (bst-in k (bst-left tree)))\n          (t (bst-in k (bst-right tree))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Insert key k into BST\n(defun bst-insert (k tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      (cons k (cons nil nil))  ; new leaf: (k nil . nil)\n    (cond ((equal k (bst-key tree)) tree)  ; already present\n          ((lexorder k (bst-key tree))\n           (cons (bst-key tree)\n                 (cons (bst-insert k (bst-left tree))\n                       (bst-right tree))))\n          (t\n           (cons (bst-key tree)\n                 (cons (bst-left tree)\n                       (bst-insert k (bst-right tree))))))))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing Our BST Operations"))

cells.append(code("; Build a BST by inserting several keys\n(defconst *bst-example*\n  (bst-insert 3\n    (bst-insert 1\n      (bst-insert 5\n        (bst-insert 2\n          (bst-insert 4 nil))))))\n\n; Test lookup and BST property\n(list (bst-in 3 *bst-example*)   ; T\n      (bst-in 7 *bst-example*)   ; NIL\n      (bst-in 1 *bst-example*)   ; T\n      (bst-p *bst-example*))     ; T"))

# 6. BST Theorems
cells.append(md("### Correctness Theorems for BSTs\n\nWe prove the two most important properties:\n1. **Preservation**: `bst-insert` preserves the BST property\n2. **Completeness**: `bst-in` finds what was inserted"))

cells.append(code("; Helper lemmas: insertion preserves bounds\n(defthm bst-all-<-of-insert\n  (implies (and (bst-all-< tree bound)\n                (lexorder k bound)\n                (not (equal k bound)))\n           (bst-all-< (bst-insert k tree) bound)))\n\n(defthm bst-all->=-of-insert\n  (implies (and (bst-all->= tree bound)\n                (lexorder bound k))\n           (bst-all->= (bst-insert k tree) bound)))"))

cells.append(code("; Main theorem: insertion preserves the BST property\n(defthm bst-p-of-bst-insert\n  (implies (bst-p tree)\n           (bst-p (bst-insert k tree))))"))

cells.append(code("; After inserting k, we can find k\n(defthm bst-in-of-bst-insert-same\n  (implies (bst-p tree)\n           (bst-in k (bst-insert k tree))))\n\n; Inserting k doesn't affect lookup of other keys\n(defthm bst-in-of-bst-insert-other\n  (implies (and (bst-p tree)\n                (not (equal j k)))\n           (equal (bst-in j (bst-insert k tree))\n                  (bst-in j tree))))"))

# 7. Balanced BSTs
cells.append(md("## 4. Balanced Binary Search Trees\n\nAn unbalanced BST can degenerate into a linked list, making operations $O(n)$. For example, inserting $1, 2, 3, 4, 5$ in order produces a right-leaning chain. To guarantee $O(\\log n)$ operations, we need *balanced* BSTs.\n\n### Leftist Trees\n\nThe ACL2 community books include a verified implementation of *leftist trees* in `books/projects/leftist-trees/`. A leftist tree satisfies:\n1. **Heap property**: parent key $\\leq$ children keys\n2. **Leftist property**: rank of left child $\\geq$ rank of right child\n\nwhere the *rank* is the length of the rightmost path to nil. The leftist property ensures the right spine is short ($O(\\log n)$), giving logarithmic merge operations.\n\n| Operation | Balanced BST | Unbalanced BST |\n|-----------|-------------|----------------|\n| Lookup    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n| Insert    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |\n| Delete    | $O(\\log n)$ | $O(n)$        |"))

cells.append(code("; Rank of a leftist tree (length of right spine)\n(defun ltree-rank (tree)\n  (if (null tree)\n      0\n    (+ 1 (ltree-rank (cddr tree)))))"))

# 8. Records/Structures
cells.append(md("## 5. Records and Structures\n\nFor modeling real-world data, we model records using alists with named fields:"))

cells.append(code("; A student record as an alist\n(defun make-student (name id gpa)\n  (list (cons 'name name)\n        (cons 'id id)\n        (cons 'gpa gpa)))\n\n(defun student-name (s) (cdr (assoc 'name s)))\n(defun student-id (s) (cdr (assoc 'id s)))\n(defun student-gpa (s) (cdr (assoc 'gpa s)))\n\n; Test\n(defconst *alice* (make-student 'alice 12345 39/10))\n(list (student-name *alice*) (student-gpa *alice*))"))

cells.append(md("### Modeling a Stack and Queue"))

cells.append(code("; Stack operations\n(defun stack-push (x stk) (cons x stk))\n(defun stack-pop (stk) (cdr stk))\n(defun stack-top (stk) (car stk))\n(defun stack-empty-p (stk) (endp stk))\n\n; Fundamental stack properties\n(defthm stack-top-of-push\n  (equal (stack-top (stack-push x stk)) x))\n\n(defthm stack-pop-of-push\n  (equal (stack-pop (stack-push x stk)) stk))"))

cells.append(code("; Queue using two lists for amortized O(1) operations\n(defun make-queue () (cons nil nil))\n\n(defun enqueue (x q)\n  (cons (car q) (cons x (cdr q))))\n\n(defun queue-flip (q)\n  (if (consp (car q))\n      q\n    (cons (reverse (cdr q)) nil)))\n\n(defun dequeue (q)\n  (let ((q2 (queue-flip q)))\n    (cons (cdar q2) (cdr q2))))\n\n(defun queue-front (q)\n  (let ((q2 (queue-flip q)))\n    (caar q2)))\n\n(defun queue-empty-p (q)\n  (and (endp (car q)) (endp (cdr q))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test the queue\n(let* ((q (make-queue))\n       (q (enqueue 'a q))\n       (q (enqueue 'b q))\n       (q (enqueue 'c q)))\n  (list (queue-front q)\n        (queue-empty-p q)))"))

# 9. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 6. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 4.1: Alist Merge\n\nDefine a function `(alist-merge a1 a2)` that merges two alists, with keys in `a1` taking precedence over keys in `a2`. Prove that for any key `k`, `(assoc k (alist-merge a1 a2))` equals `(assoc k a1)` when present in `a1`, and `(assoc k a2)` otherwise."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 4.1: Define alist-merge and prove its correctness\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 4.2: BST Minimum\n\nDefine a function `(bst-min tree)` that returns the minimum key in a non-empty BST. Prove that the minimum is always found in the leftmost path.\n\n*Hint:* In a BST, the minimum is the leftmost leaf."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 4.2: Define bst-min and prove its properties\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 4.3: BST Deletion\n\nDefine a function `(bst-delete k tree)` that removes key `k` from a BST while maintaining the BST property. You will need a helper to find the minimum of the right subtree when the deleted node has two children.\n\nProve:\n1. `(bst-p (bst-delete k tree))` when `(bst-p tree)`\n2. `(not (bst-in k (bst-delete k tree)))` when `(bst-p tree)`"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 4.3: Define bst-delete and prove its properties\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 4.4: BST to Sorted List\n\nDefine a function `(bst-to-list tree)` that performs an in-order traversal and returns a sorted list of all keys. Prove that the result is sorted (each element is $\\leq$ the next)."))

# Summary and next
cells.append(md("## Summary\n\nIn this module, we learned:\n\n- **Alists** provide simple key-value storage with $O(n)$ lookup\n- **Binary search trees** improve lookup to $O(\\log n)$ when balanced\n- We can *prove* that BST operations preserve structural invariants\n- **Balanced trees** (like leftist trees) guarantee logarithmic performance\n- **Records, stacks, and queues** can all be modeled with cons-based structures\n- Every data structure follows the pattern: representation → recognizer → operations → theorems\n\nThe key insight is that in ACL2, data structure correctness is not just tested but *formally verified*."))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 5: Graph Algorithms\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–4 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction, Data Structures)\n\nGraphs are one of the most versatile data structures in computer science. In this module, we represent graphs in ACL2, implement classic graph algorithms (DFS, BFS, Dijkstra), and *prove* their correctness properties."))

# 2. Graph Representation
cells.append(md("## 1. Graph Representation\n\nWe represent graphs as *adjacency lists* — an alist mapping each node to its list of neighbors.\n\nFor an **unweighted** graph:\n```\n((A . (B C))     ; A connects to B and C\n (B . (A D))     ; B connects to A and D\n ...)\n```\n\nFor a **weighted** graph (used in Dijkstra's algorithm):\n```\n((A . ((B . 4) (C . 2)))    ; A→B cost 4, A→C cost 2\n (B . ((D . 3)))             ; B→D cost 3\n ...)\n```"))

cells.append(code("; Recognizer for an unweighted graph (alist of node -> neighbor list)\n(defun graphp (g)\n  (if (endp g)\n      t\n    (and (consp (car g))\n         (true-listp (cdar g))\n         (graphp (cdr g)))))\n\n; Get the neighbors of a node in graph g\n(defun neighbors (node g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal node g)))\n\n; Get all nodes in the graph\n(defun nodes (g)\n  (strip-cars g))\n\n; Is there an edge from u to v?\n(defun edge-p (u v g)\n  (member-equal v (neighbors u g)))"))

cells.append(md("### Example Graph\n\nLet's define a sample undirected graph:\n\n```\n    A --- B\n    |     |\n    C --- D --- E\n```"))

cells.append(code("; An undirected graph — each edge appears in both directions\n(defconst *example-graph*\n  '((a . (b c))\n    (b . (a d))\n    (c . (a d))\n    (d . (b c e))\n    (e . (d))))\n\n; Test the graph operations\n(list (graphp *example-graph*)\n      (neighbors 'd *example-graph*)\n      (nodes *example-graph*)\n      (edge-p 'a 'b *example-graph*)\n      (edge-p 'a 'e *example-graph*))"))

# 3. DFS
cells.append(md("## 2. Graph Traversal: Depth-First Search (DFS)\n\nDFS explores a graph by going as deep as possible along each branch before backtracking. We use the *accumulator pattern* to track visited nodes.\n\n- Maintain a `visited` list to avoid revisiting nodes\n- For each unvisited neighbor, recursively visit it\n- The accumulator grows monotonically (we only add, never remove)"))

cells.append(code("; Depth-First Search\n; Returns the list of all nodes reachable from 'start'\n(defun dfs-visit (node g visited)\n  (if (member-equal node visited)\n      visited\n    (dfs-visit-neighbors (neighbors node g)\n                         g\n                         (cons node visited))))\n\n(defun dfs-visit-neighbors (nbrs g visited)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      visited\n    (dfs-visit-neighbors (cdr nbrs)\n                         g\n                         (dfs-visit (car nbrs) g visited))))"))

cells.append(code("; Run DFS starting from node A\n(dfs-visit 'a *example-graph* nil)"))

cells.append(md("### DFS Properties"))

cells.append(code("; Property: visited list only grows (monotonicity)\n(defthm dfs-visit-monotonic\n  (subsetp-equal visited\n                 (dfs-visit node g visited)))\n\n; Property: the start node is in the result\n(defthm start-in-dfs\n  (member-equal node\n                (dfs-visit node g visited)))"))

# 4. BFS
cells.append(md("## 3. Breadth-First Search (BFS)\n\nBFS explores a graph level by level using a queue. It visits all neighbors of the current node before going deeper.\n\n| Property | DFS | BFS |\n|----------|-----|-----|\n| Data structure | Stack (call stack) | Queue |\n| Order | Deep first | Broad first |\n| Shortest path? | No | Yes (unweighted) |\n| Space | $O(\\text{depth})$ | $O(\\text{width})$ |"))

cells.append(code("; Breadth-First Search using a queue\n(defun bfs-step (queue g visited)\n  (declare (xargs :measure (let ((unseen (set-difference-equal (nodes g) visited)))\n                             (+ (len unseen) (len queue)))))\n  (if (endp queue)\n      visited\n    (let ((node (car queue))\n          (rest (cdr queue)))\n      (if (member-equal node visited)\n          (bfs-step rest g visited)\n        (let ((new-nbrs (set-difference-equal\n                          (neighbors node g)\n                          visited)))\n          (bfs-step (append rest new-nbrs)\n                    g\n                    (cons node visited)))))))\n\n; BFS entry point\n(defun bfs (start g)\n  (bfs-step (list start) g nil))"))

cells.append(code("; Run BFS from node A\n(bfs 'a *example-graph*)"))

cells.append(md("In an unweighted graph, BFS visits nodes in order of their distance from the start. The first time BFS reaches a node, it has found the shortest path (fewest edges)."))

# 5. Dijkstra
cells.append(md("## 4. Shortest Paths: Dijkstra's Algorithm\n\nFor *weighted* graphs, we need Dijkstra's algorithm. The ACL2 community books contain a fully verified implementation in `books/misc/dijkstra-shortest-path.lisp` with 126 theorems. We present a simplified version here.\n\n**Key Concepts:**\n- **Distance table**: alist mapping each node to its current best known distance\n- **Relaxation**: when we find a shorter path, update the distance\n- **Priority selection**: always process the unvisited node with smallest distance"))

cells.append(code("; Weighted graph\n(defconst *weighted-graph*\n  '((a . ((b . 4) (c . 2)))\n    (b . ((d . 3) (e . 1)))\n    (c . ((b . 1) (d . 5)))\n    (d . ((e . 2)))\n    (e . ())))\n\n; Get weighted neighbors and edge weight\n(defun weighted-neighbors (node g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal node g)))\n\n(defun edge-weight (u v g)\n  (cdr (assoc-equal v (weighted-neighbors u g))))"))

cells.append(code("; Distance table operations ('inf for infinity)\n(defun get-dist (node dist-table)\n  (let ((pair (assoc-equal node dist-table)))\n    (if pair (cdr pair) 'inf)))\n\n(defun set-dist (node d dist-table)\n  (acons node d dist-table))\n\n(defun dist< (a b)\n  (cond ((equal b 'inf) (not (equal a 'inf)))\n        ((equal a 'inf) nil)\n        (t (< a b))))"))

cells.append(code("; Relax a single edge and all neighbors of a node\n(defun relax-edge (u v w dist-table)\n  (let ((du (get-dist u dist-table))\n        (dv (get-dist v dist-table)))\n    (if (and (not (equal du 'inf))\n             (dist< (+ du w) dv))\n        (set-dist v (+ du w) dist-table)\n      dist-table)))\n\n(defun relax-neighbors (u nbrs dist-table)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      dist-table\n    (let* ((v (caar nbrs))\n           (w (cdar nbrs))\n           (new-dist (relax-edge u v w dist-table)))\n      (relax-neighbors u (cdr nbrs) new-dist))))"))

cells.append(code("; Find the unvisited node with smallest distance\n(defun find-min-node (nodes dist-table visited)\n  (if (endp nodes)\n      nil\n    (let ((node (car nodes)))\n      (if (member-equal node visited)\n          (find-min-node (cdr nodes) dist-table visited)\n        (let ((rest-min (find-min-node (cdr nodes) dist-table visited)))\n          (if (null rest-min)\n              node\n            (if (dist< (get-dist node dist-table)\n                       (get-dist rest-min dist-table))\n                node\n              rest-min)))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Main loop: pick min node, relax, repeat\n(defun dijkstra-step (g all-nodes dist-table visited)\n  (let ((u (find-min-node all-nodes dist-table visited)))\n    (if (null u)\n        (mv dist-table visited)\n      (let* ((nbrs (weighted-neighbors u g))\n             (new-dist (relax-neighbors u nbrs dist-table))\n             (new-visited (cons u visited)))\n        (dijkstra-step g all-nodes new-dist new-visited)))))\n\n; Dijkstra entry point\n(defun dijkstra (source g)\n  (let* ((all-nodes (strip-cars g))\n         (dist-table (set-dist source 0 nil)))\n    (dijkstra-step g all-nodes dist-table nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Run Dijkstra from node A\n(dijkstra 'a *weighted-graph*)"))

cells.append(md("The full correctness proof establishes **optimality** (distances are truly shortest), the **triangle inequality** ($d(v) \\leq d(u) + w$), and **termination** (the visited set grows at each step)."))

# 6. Path Finding
cells.append(md("## 5. Path Finding\n\nBeyond distances, we often need actual paths. We define a path predicate and a path-finding function:"))

cells.append(code("; A path is valid if consecutive nodes are connected by edges\n(defun pathp (path g)\n  (cond ((endp path) t)\n        ((endp (cdr path)) (member-equal (car path) (nodes g)))\n        (t (and (edge-p (car path) (cadr path) g)\n                (pathp (cdr path) g)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Find a path from start to end using DFS\n(defun find-path (start end g visited)\n  (cond ((equal start end) (list start))\n        ((member-equal start visited) nil)\n        (t (find-path-neighbors\n             (neighbors start g)\n             start end g\n             (cons start visited)))))\n\n(defun find-path-neighbors (nbrs start end g visited)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      nil\n    (let ((sub-path (find-path (car nbrs) end g visited)))\n      (if sub-path\n          (cons start sub-path)\n        (find-path-neighbors (cdr nbrs) start end g visited)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Find a path from A to E and verify it\n(let ((p (find-path 'a 'e *example-graph* nil)))\n  (list p (pathp p *example-graph*)))"))

cells.append(code("; The path returned by find-path (if non-nil) is valid\n(defthm find-path-is-valid\n  (implies (find-path start end g visited)\n           (pathp (find-path start end g visited) g)))"))

# 7. Cycle Detection
cells.append(md("## 6. Cycle Detection\n\nA *cycle* is a path that starts and ends at the same node. The classic DFS-based detection uses three colors:\n- **White**: not yet visited\n- **Gray**: currently being explored (on the DFS stack)\n- **Black**: fully explored\n\nA *back edge* (an edge to a gray node) indicates a cycle in a directed graph."))

cells.append(code("; Color map operations\n(defun get-color (node colors)\n  (let ((pair (assoc-equal node colors)))\n    (if pair (cdr pair) 'white)))\n\n(defun set-color (node color colors)\n  (acons node color colors))"))

cells.append(code("; DFS-based cycle detection for directed graphs\n(defun detect-cycle-visit (node g colors)\n  (let ((colors (set-color node 'gray colors)))\n    (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n      (detect-cycle-neighbors (neighbors node g) g colors)\n      (if cycle-found\n          (mv t colors)\n        (mv nil (set-color node 'black colors))))))\n\n(defun detect-cycle-neighbors (nbrs g colors)\n  (if (endp nbrs)\n      (mv nil colors)\n    (let ((v (car nbrs))\n          (c (get-color (car nbrs) colors)))\n      (cond\n        ((equal c 'gray) (mv t colors))  ; back edge = cycle!\n        ((equal c 'white)\n         (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n           (detect-cycle-visit v g colors)\n           (if cycle-found\n               (mv t colors)\n             (detect-cycle-neighbors (cdr nbrs) g colors))))\n        (t (detect-cycle-neighbors (cdr nbrs) g colors))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Check all nodes for cycles\n(defun has-cycle (g)\n  (has-cycle-nodes (nodes g) g nil))\n\n(defun has-cycle-nodes (node-list g colors)\n  (if (endp node-list)\n      nil\n    (if (equal (get-color (car node-list) colors) 'white)\n        (mv-let (cycle-found colors)\n          (detect-cycle-visit (car node-list) g colors)\n          (if cycle-found\n              t\n            (has-cycle-nodes (cdr node-list) g colors)))\n      (has-cycle-nodes (cdr node-list) g colors))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: cyclic vs acyclic graphs\n(defconst *cyclic-graph*\n  '((a . (b)) (b . (c)) (c . (a))))\n\n(defconst *dag*\n  '((a . (b c)) (b . (d)) (c . (d)) (d . ())))\n\n(list (has-cycle *cyclic-graph*)   ; T\n      (has-cycle *dag*))           ; NIL"))

# 8. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 7. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 5.1: Connected Components\n\nDefine a function `(connected-components g)` that returns a list of lists, where each inner list is the set of nodes in one connected component of an undirected graph.\n\n*Hint:* Use DFS from each unvisited node to collect one component."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 5.1: Connected components\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 5.2: Topological Sort\n\nDefine a function `(topological-sort g)` that returns a topological ordering of a DAG — every node appears before all nodes it has edges to.\n\n*Hint:* Use DFS and add each node to the result *after* visiting all its neighbors (reverse postorder)."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 5.2: Topological sort\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 5.3: Shortest Path Reconstruction\n\nExtend Dijkstra's algorithm to compute a *predecessor table*, then define `(reconstruct-path pred-table source dest)` that returns the actual shortest path. Prove the reconstructed path is valid."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 5.3: Dijkstra with path reconstruction\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("## Summary\n\nIn this module, we learned to:\n\n- **Represent graphs** as adjacency lists (alists) in ACL2\n- **Traverse graphs** with DFS (stack-based) and BFS (queue-based)\n- **Find shortest paths** using Dijkstra's algorithm with distance table relaxation\n- **Find paths** and prove they are valid\n- **Detect cycles** using the white-gray-black DFS coloring technique\n\n| Proof Technique | Used For |\n|----------------|----------|\n| Accumulator monotonicity | DFS visited list only grows |\n| Measure functions | Proving BFS/DFS terminate |\n| Invariant maintenance | Dijkstra's distance table properties |\n| Structural induction | Properties over graph structure |"))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
