cells = []

# 1. Title
cells.append(md("# Module 6: Machine Models — The M1 Stack Machine\n\n**Prerequisites:** Modules 1–5 (Introduction, Logic & Proofs, Recursion & Induction, Data Structures, Graph Algorithms)\n\nIn this module, we study how to *model a computer* inside a theorem prover and *prove that programs are correct*. We use the M1 machine — a simplified JVM-like stack machine designed by J Strother Moore for teaching program verification with ACL2. The M1 machine is defined in `books/demos/marktoberdorf-08/m1.lisp`."))

# 2. Why Machine Models?
cells.append(md("## 1. Why Machine Models?\n\nSoftware verification ultimately requires a *formal model of computation*. Without one, we can prove properties of mathematical functions, but not that an *actual program* computes the right answer.\n\nA machine model gives us:\n- A precise definition of what each instruction does\n- A state that we can reason about formally\n- A `step` function that describes one execution step\n- A `run` function that executes multiple steps\n\n### The Verification Pipeline\n\n$$\\text{Specification (math function)} \\xleftarrow{\\text{prove } =} \\text{Program on Machine Model} \\xrightarrow{\\text{compile}} \\text{Real Hardware}$$"))

# 3. The M1 Machine
cells.append(md("## 2. The M1 Machine\n\nM1 is a tiny JVM-like stack machine with:\n- A **program counter** (pc): index into the instruction list\n- **Local variables**: a list of values, accessed by index\n- An **operand stack**: used for arithmetic and comparisons\n- A **program**: a list of instructions\n\n### Instruction Set\n\n| Instruction | Effect | Stack |\n|-------------|--------|-------|\n| `(ICONST k)` | Push constant $k$ | $\\ldots \\Rightarrow \\ldots, k$ |\n| `(ILOAD i)` | Push `locals[i]` | $\\ldots \\Rightarrow \\ldots, v$ |\n| `(ISTORE i)` | Pop into `locals[i]` | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IADD)` | Pop two, push sum | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a+b$ |\n| `(ISUB)` | Pop two, push difference | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a-b$ |\n| `(IMUL)` | Pop two, push product | $\\ldots, a, b \\Rightarrow \\ldots, a \\times b$ |\n| `(IFEQ off)` | Pop; if 0, jump | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IFLE off)` | Pop; if $\\leq 0$, jump | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(IFLT off)` | Pop; if $< 0$, jump | $\\ldots, v \\Rightarrow \\ldots$ |\n| `(GOTO off)` | Unconditional jump | (unchanged) |\n| `(HALT)` | Stop execution | (unchanged) |"))

# 4. Defining the Machine
cells.append(md("## 3. Defining the Machine\n\n### State Representation and Accessors"))

cells.append(code("; M1 State constructor and accessors\n(defun make-state (pc locals stack program)\n  (list pc locals stack program))\n\n(defun pc (s) (nth 0 s))\n(defun locals (s) (nth 1 s))\n(defun stack (s) (nth 2 s))\n(defun program (s) (nth 3 s))"))

cells.append(code("; Stack operations for M1\n(defun push-stack (v stk) (cons v stk))\n(defun top-stack (stk) (car stk))\n(defun pop-stack (stk) (cdr stk))\n\n; Instruction fetch and decode\n(defun current-instruction (s)\n  (nth (pc s) (program s)))\n\n(defun opcode (inst) (car inst))\n(defun arg1 (inst) (cadr inst))\n\n; Local variable access\n(defun local-var (i locals)\n  (nth i locals))\n\n(defun update-local (i v locals)\n  (update-nth i v locals))"))

cells.append(md("### The Step Function\n\nThe heart of M1 — executes one instruction and returns the new state:"))

cells.append(code("; Execute one instruction\n(defun execute-instruction (inst s)\n  (let ((op (opcode inst)))\n    (case op\n      (ICONST\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (arg1 inst) (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ILOAD\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (local-var (arg1 inst) (locals s))\n                               (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ISTORE\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (update-local (arg1 inst)\n                                 (top-stack (stack s))\n                                 (locals s))\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IADD\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (+ (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (ISUB\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (- (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IMUL\n       (make-state (+ 1 (pc s))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (push-stack (* (top-stack (pop-stack (stack s)))\n                                  (top-stack (stack s)))\n                               (pop-stack (pop-stack (stack s))))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFEQ\n       (make-state (if (equal (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFLE\n       (make-state (if (<= (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (IFLT\n       (make-state (if (< (top-stack (stack s)) 0)\n                       (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                     (+ 1 (pc s)))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (pop-stack (stack s))\n                   (program s)))\n      (GOTO\n       (make-state (+ (pc s) (arg1 inst))\n                   (locals s)\n                   (stack s)\n                   (program s)))\n      (HALT s)\n      (otherwise s))))"))

cells.append(code("; Take one step (if not halted)\n(defun m1-step (s)\n  (let ((inst (current-instruction s)))\n    (if (equal (opcode inst) 'HALT)\n        s\n      (execute-instruction inst s))))\n\n; Run for n steps (the 'clock')\n(defun run (s n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      s\n    (run (m1-step s) (- n 1))))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing the Machine"))

cells.append(code("; Simple program: compute 3 + 4\n(defconst *add-program*\n  '((ICONST 3)\n    (ICONST 4)\n    (IADD)\n    (HALT)))\n\n; Run 3 steps and check the result\n(let ((result (run (make-state 0 nil nil *add-program*) 3)))\n  (top-stack (stack result)))  ; should be 7"))

# 5. Factorial Example
cells.append(md("## 4. Example: Factorial Program\n\n### Mathematical Specification"))

cells.append(code("; Mathematical factorial\n(defun fact (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      1\n    (* n (fact (- n 1)))))\n\n(list (fact 0) (fact 1) (fact 5) (fact 10))"))

cells.append(md("### Factorial as M1 Bytecode\n\nLocal variables: `locals[0]` = n (counts down), `locals[1]` = acc (starts at 1).\n\nAlgorithm: `acc = 1; while (n > 0) { acc = acc * n; n = n - 1; }`\n\n```\npc  Instruction       Comment\n--  -----------       -------\n 0  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 1  (IFEQ 10)         if n == 0, jump to pc 11\n 2  (ILOAD 1)         push acc\n 3  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 4  (IMUL)            acc * n\n 5  (ISTORE 1)        store into acc\n 6  (ILOAD 0)         push n\n 7  (ICONST 1)        push 1\n 8  (ISUB)            n - 1\n 9  (ISTORE 0)        store into n\n10  (GOTO -10)        jump back to pc 0\n11  (ILOAD 1)         push result\n12  (HALT)\n```"))

cells.append(code("; Factorial program for M1\n(defconst *factorial-program*\n  '((ILOAD 0)          ; 0: push n\n    (IFEQ 10)          ; 1: if n=0 goto 11\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 2: push acc\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 3: push n\n    (IMUL)             ; 4: acc*n\n    (ISTORE 1)         ; 5: acc = acc*n\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 6: push n\n    (ICONST 1)         ; 7: push 1\n    (ISUB)             ; 8: n-1\n    (ISTORE 0)         ; 9: n = n-1\n    (GOTO -10)         ; 10: goto 0\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 11: push result\n    (HALT)))           ; 12: stop"))

cells.append(code("; Run factorial(5): initial state pc=0, locals=(5 1), stack=nil\n(defconst *fact-5-result*\n  (run (make-state 0 '(5 1) nil *factorial-program*) 100))\n\n; The answer should be 120 = 5!\n(list (top-stack (stack *fact-5-result*))\n      (fact 5)\n      (equal (top-stack (stack *fact-5-result*)) (fact 5)))"))

cells.append(md("### Step-by-Step Execution"))

cells.append(code("; Trace the first few steps for factorial(3)\n(let ((s0 (make-state 0 '(3 1) nil *factorial-program*)))\n  (list\n    (list 'step-0 s0)\n    (list 'step-1 (run s0 1))    ; ILOAD 0: push 3\n    (list 'step-2 (run s0 2))    ; IFEQ: 3≠0, continue\n    (list 'step-3 (run s0 3))    ; ILOAD 1: push acc=1\n    (list 'step-4 (run s0 4))    ; ILOAD 0: push n=3\n    (list 'step-5 (run s0 5))    ; IMUL: 1*3=3\n    (list 'step-6 (run s0 6))))  ; ISTORE 1: acc=3"))

# 6. Iterative Sum
cells.append(md("## 5. Example: Iterative Sum\n\nCompute $\\sum_{i=1}^{n} i = \\frac{n(n+1)}{2}$."))

cells.append(code("; Mathematical sum from 1 to n\n(defun sum-1-to-n (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      0\n    (+ n (sum-1-to-n (- n 1)))))\n\n; The closed form\n(defthm sum-closed-form\n  (implies (natp n)\n           (equal (sum-1-to-n n)\n                  (/ (* n (+ n 1)) 2))))"))

cells.append(code("; Sum program: locals[0]=n (down-counter), locals[1]=sum (accumulator)\n(defconst *sum-program*\n  '((ILOAD 0)          ; 0: push n\n    (IFEQ 10)          ; 1: if n=0 goto 11\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 2: push sum\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 3: push n\n    (IADD)             ; 4: sum + n\n    (ISTORE 1)         ; 5: sum = sum + n\n    (ILOAD 0)          ; 6: push n\n    (ICONST 1)         ; 7: push 1\n    (ISUB)             ; 8: n - 1\n    (ISTORE 0)         ; 9: n = n - 1\n    (GOTO -10)         ; 10: goto 0\n    (ILOAD 1)          ; 11: push result\n    (HALT)))           ; 12: stop\n\n; Run sum(10) and verify\n(let ((result (run (make-state 0 '(10 0) nil *sum-program*) 200)))\n  (list (top-stack (stack result))\n        (sum-1-to-n 10)\n        (/ (* 10 11) 2)))"))

# 7. Clock Functions
cells.append(md("## 6. Clock Functions\n\nThe key proof technique for M1 programs is the *clock function*. A clock function tells us exactly how many steps a program needs to reach completion.\n\nTo prove `(run state n)` produces the right answer, we need:\n1. The right number of steps `n` (the *clock*)\n2. A proof that after `n` steps, the result is correct"))

cells.append(code("; Clock function for the factorial program\n; Each loop iteration takes 11 steps, final exit takes 2\n(defun fact-clock (n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      2  ; test n=0, load result, halt\n    (+ 11 (fact-clock (- n 1)))))\n\n; How many steps for factorial(5)?\n(fact-clock 5)"))

cells.append(code("; The M1 factorial function: run with the correct clock\n(defun m1-factorial (n)\n  (top-stack\n    (stack\n      (run (make-state 0 (list n 1) nil *factorial-program*)\n           (fact-clock n)))))\n\n; Test that m1-factorial matches fact\n(list (equal (m1-factorial 0) (fact 0))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 1) (fact 1))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 5) (fact 5))\n      (equal (m1-factorial 10) (fact 10)))"))

cells.append(md("### The Correctness Theorem\n\n$$\\forall n \\in \\mathbb{N}: \\text{m1-factorial}(n) = n!$$"))

cells.append(code("; M1 factorial equals mathematical factorial\n(defthm m1-factorial-is-fact\n  (implies (natp n)\n           (equal (m1-factorial n)\n                  (fact n))))"))

# 8. Loop Invariants
cells.append(md("## 7. Loop Invariants in M1\n\n### The Classical Approach\n\nProving a loop correct requires a *loop invariant* — a property that holds at each iteration:\n1. **Initialization**: holds when the loop starts\n2. **Maintenance**: preserved by each iteration\n3. **Termination**: combined with exit condition, implies the postcondition\n\nThis is the Hoare logic approach: $\\{\\text{Inv} \\land \\text{condition}\\} \\;\\text{body}\\; \\{\\text{Inv}\\}$\n\n### Factorial Loop Invariant\n\nAt pc=0: $\\text{acc} \\times n! = N!$ where $N$ is the original input.\n\n- **Init**: $\\text{acc} = 1, n = N \\Rightarrow 1 \\times N! = N!$ ✓\n- **Maintenance**: $\\text{acc}' = \\text{acc} \\times n, n' = n-1 \\Rightarrow (\\text{acc} \\times n) \\times (n-1)! = \\text{acc} \\times n! = N!$ ✓\n- **Termination**: $n = 0 \\Rightarrow \\text{acc} \\times 0! = \\text{acc} = N!$ ✓"))

cells.append(code("; Loop invariant: acc * fact(n) = fact(N)\n(defun fact-loop-invariant (n acc)\n  (* acc (fact n)))\n\n; The invariant is preserved by one iteration\n(defthm fact-invariant-maintained\n  (implies (and (natp n) (not (zp n)))\n           (equal (fact-loop-invariant (- n 1) (* acc n))\n                  (fact-loop-invariant n acc))))\n\n; At termination (n=0), the invariant gives the answer\n(defthm fact-invariant-at-exit\n  (equal (fact-loop-invariant 0 acc)\n         acc))"))

cells.append(md("### Connection to Hoare Logic\n\n| Hoare Logic | M1 Approach |\n|---|---|\n| Precondition $\\{P\\}$ | Initial state conditions |\n| Postcondition $\\{Q\\}$ | Properties of final state |\n| Loop invariant | Invariant at loop head (pc=0) |\n| Weakest precondition | Clock function derivation |\n| Proof rule for loops | Induction on clock |\n\nThe advantage of M1: everything is mechanically checked by ACL2 — no gaps in reasoning."))

cells.append(md("### Verification Methodology Summary\n\n1. **Write the program** as M1 bytecode\n2. **Define the specification** as a mathematical function\n3. **Define the clock function** computing steps needed\n4. **State the loop invariant** relating machine state to specification\n5. **Prove the invariant is maintained** through each iteration\n6. **Prove the final theorem**: running with the clock gives the specification"))

# 9. Exercises
cells.append(md("## 8. Exercises\n\n### Exercise 6.1: Multiplication by Repeated Addition\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes $a \\times b$ using repeated addition (no IMUL). Use locals[0]=a, locals[1]=b (counts down), locals[2]=result. Define the clock function and prove correctness."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.1: Multiplication by repeated addition\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 6.2: Fibonacci\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes the $n$-th Fibonacci number. Use locals[0]=n (counter), locals[1]=fib(k), locals[2]=fib(k-1). Prove your program matches the mathematical Fibonacci."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.2: Fibonacci on M1\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 6.3: Exponentiation\n\nWrite an M1 program that computes $b^n$ using repeated multiplication. Define the clock function and prove correctness.\n\n*Bonus:* Write a fast-exponentiation version using squaring. What does the clock function look like?"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.3: Exponentiation on M1\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 6.4: Verify Your Own Program\n\nChoose a simple algorithm (GCD, integer division, counting) and:\n1. Write it as M1 bytecode\n2. Define its mathematical specification\n3. Define the clock function\n4. State and prove the loop invariant\n5. Prove the correctness theorem"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 6.4: Your own M1 program\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n"))

# Summary
cells.append(md("## Summary\n\nIn this module, we learned:\n\n- **Machine models** provide a formal foundation for program verification\n- **M1** is a simple JVM-like stack machine with 11 instructions\n- The **state** is `(pc, locals, stack, program)`\n- **Clock functions** determine how many steps a program needs\n- **Loop invariants** are the key technique for verifying loops\n- The methodology connects directly to **Hoare logic**\n\n| Module | What We Verified |\n|--------|------------------|\n| 2–3 | Mathematical functions (factorial, append, etc.) |\n| 4 | Data structure operations (BST insert, lookup) |\n| 5 | Graph algorithms (DFS, Dijkstra) |\n| **6** | **Programs running on a machine model** |\n\n$$\\text{Math} \\rightarrow \\text{Data Structures} \\rightarrow \\text{Algorithms} \\rightarrow \\text{Programs}$$"))

You now have the foundations to explore:\n\n- **Compiler verification**: Proving a compiler correctly translates to M1 bytecode\n- **Operating system verification**: Modeling process scheduling and memory management\n- **Hardware verification**: Using ACL2 to verify processor designs (as done at AMD)\n- **Cryptographic verification**: Proving properties of cryptographic algorithms\n\nThe ACL2 community books contain thousands of verified programs, algorithms, and machine models to learn from."))

    md("### Understanding the Clock\n\nThe clock function reflects the program's control flow:\n- If $n = 0$: we execute 2 instructions (test $n$, load result + halt)\n- Each iteration: 11 instructions (test, load, load, add, store, load, push, sub, store, goto, then re-test)\n- Total for input $n$: $11n + 2$ steps\n\nThe clock function is a *constructive termination argument* — it proves the program terminates by giving an exact step count."),

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. INTRODUCTION
cells.append(md("# Module 7: Hardware Verification\n\n**ACL2 for Computer Science — University-Level Tutorial Series**"))

cells.append(md("## Learning Objectives\n\nBy the end of this module you will be able to:\n\n1. Model basic digital logic gates as ACL2 functions\n2. Build and verify combinational circuits (adders)\n3. Represent multi-bit values as boolean vectors and reason about them\n4. Model sequential circuits with state\n5. Appreciate how these techniques scale to real processor verification (FM9001)"))

cells.append(md("## 7.1 Introduction — Why Hardware Verification?\n\nHardware verification is one of ACL2's **flagship applications**. The most celebrated result is the **FM9001**: a complete microprocessor whose design was formally verified end-to-end in ACL2 by Warren Hunt and colleagues.\n\nWhy does hardware need formal verification?\n\n- A single bug in a processor can cost billions (recall the Intel FDIV bug, ~\\$475 million)\n- Hardware cannot be \"patched\" after fabrication\n- Exhaustive testing is infeasible for circuits with $2^{64}$ or more input combinations\n\nDigital circuits can be modeled as **boolean functions**: they take boolean inputs and produce boolean outputs. This makes them a natural fit for theorem proving."))

cells.append(md("### The Verification Approach\n\nThe basic methodology is:\n\n1. **Model** the hardware design as ACL2 functions\n2. **Specify** the intended behavior as a separate ACL2 function\n3. **Prove** that the model implements the specification\n\nWe start with the simplest building blocks — logic gates — and work our way up to multi-bit circuits."))

# 2. BOOLEAN GATES
cells.append(md("## 7.2 Boolean Gates in ACL2\n\nIn ACL2, we represent boolean values as `t` (true) and `nil` (false). We can model the fundamental logic gates using nested `if` expressions.\n\nRecall that `(if test then else)` returns `then` when `test` is non-nil, and `else` when `test` is nil."))

cells.append(code("; Boolean AND gate\n(defun b-and (a b)\n  (if a (if b t nil) nil))"))

cells.append(code("; Boolean OR gate\n(defun b-or (a b)\n  (if a t (if b t nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Boolean NOT gate\n(defun b-not (a)\n  (if a nil t))"))

cells.append(code("; Boolean XOR gate\n(defun b-xor (a b)\n  (if a (if b nil t) (if b t nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Boolean NAND gate\n(defun b-nand (a b)\n  (b-not (b-and a b)))"))

cells.append(code("; Boolean NOR gate\n(defun b-nor (a b)\n  (b-not (b-or a b)))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing Our Gates\n\nLet's verify our gate definitions match the expected truth tables:"))

cells.append(code("; AND truth table\n(list (b-and nil nil)   ; nil\n      (b-and nil t)     ; nil\n      (b-and t nil)     ; nil\n      (b-and t t))      ; t"))

cells.append(code("; XOR truth table\n(list (b-xor nil nil)   ; nil\n      (b-xor nil t)     ; t\n      (b-xor t nil)     ; t\n      (b-xor t t))      ; nil"))

cells.append(md("### De Morgan's Laws\n\nDe Morgan's laws are fundamental identities in boolean algebra:\n\n$$\\neg(A \\wedge B) = \\neg A \\vee \\neg B$$\n$$\\neg(A \\vee B) = \\neg A \\wedge \\neg B$$\n\nLet's prove these for our gate definitions. Since our inputs are booleans (either `t` or `nil`), ACL2 can verify these by case analysis."))

cells.append(code("; De Morgan's first law: NOT(A AND B) = (NOT A) OR (NOT B)\n(thm\n  (equal (b-not (b-and a b))\n         (b-or (b-not a) (b-not b))))"))

cells.append(code("; De Morgan's second law: NOT(A OR B) = (NOT A) AND (NOT B)\n(thm\n  (equal (b-not (b-or a b))\n         (b-and (b-not a) (b-not b))))"))

cells.append(code("; XOR self-inverse property: a XOR a = false\n(thm\n  (equal (b-xor a a) nil))"))

cells.append(code("; XOR with nil is identity\n(thm\n  (equal (b-xor a nil) (if a t nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; NAND is universal: we can build NOT from NAND\n(thm\n  (equal (b-nand a a)\n         (b-not a)))"))

# 3. COMBINATIONAL CIRCUITS
cells.append(md("## 7.3 Combinational Circuits — The Full Adder\n\nA **combinational circuit** computes outputs purely from its current inputs (no memory or state). The most fundamental arithmetic circuit is the **full adder**.\n\nA 1-bit full adder takes three inputs — `a`, `b`, and `cin` (carry-in) — and produces two outputs:\n- **sum**: the low bit of $a + b + c_{in}$\n- **carry**: the high bit of $a + b + c_{in}$\n\n$$\\text{sum} = a \\oplus b \\oplus c_{in}$$\n$$\\text{carry} = (a \\wedge b) \\vee (c_{in} \\wedge (a \\oplus b))$$"))

cells.append(code("; Full adder: sum output\n(defun full-adder-sum (a b cin)\n  (b-xor (b-xor a b) cin))"))

cells.append(code("; Full adder: carry output\n(defun full-adder-carry (a b cin)\n  (b-or (b-and a b)\n        (b-and cin (b-xor a b))))"))

cells.append(md("### Correctness of the Full Adder\n\nTo state correctness, we need a way to convert booleans to numbers. We define a helper that maps `t` to 1 and `nil` to 0:"))

cells.append(code("; Convert a boolean to a natural number\n(defun bool-to-nat (x)\n  (if x 1 0))"))

cells.append(code("; The full adder is correct: the two-bit output (carry, sum)\n; represents the arithmetic sum a + b + cin.\n(thm\n  (equal (+ (* 2 (bool-to-nat (full-adder-carry a b cin)))\n            (bool-to-nat (full-adder-sum a b cin)))\n         (+ (bool-to-nat a)\n            (bool-to-nat b)\n            (bool-to-nat cin))))"))

cells.append(md("This theorem states that the numeric value of the two-bit result (carry as the high bit, sum as the low bit) equals the arithmetic sum of the three input bits. ACL2 proves this automatically by case analysis."))

cells.append(md("### Ripple-Carry Adder\n\nWe can chain full adders to add multi-bit numbers. Each adder's carry-out feeds into the next adder's carry-in, forming a **ripple-carry adder**.\n\nWe represent multi-bit numbers as lists of booleans (least-significant bit first)."))

cells.append(code("; Ripple-carry adder: adds two bit-vectors with a carry-in.\n; Returns a list of sum bits (least-significant first).\n(defun ripple-carry-adder (x y cin)\n  (if (and (endp x) (endp y))\n      (if cin (list t) nil)\n    (let* ((a (if (consp x) (car x) nil))\n           (b (if (consp y) (car y) nil))\n           (sum-bit (full-adder-sum a b cin))\n           (carry-out (full-adder-carry a b cin)))\n      (cons sum-bit\n            (ripple-carry-adder (cdr x) (cdr y) carry-out)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: 3 + 5 = 8 in 4-bit binary\n; 3 = (t t nil nil), 5 = (t nil t nil)\n; 8 = (nil nil nil t)\n(ripple-carry-adder (list t t nil nil)\n                    (list t nil t nil)\n                    nil)"))

# 4. BIT VECTORS
cells.append(md("## 7.4 Bit Vectors\n\nTo reason about multi-bit hardware, we represent values as **bit vectors** — lists of booleans with the least-significant bit first.\n\nWe need conversion functions between bit vectors and natural numbers:"))

cells.append(code("; Convert a bit vector (LSB first) to a natural number\n(defun bv-to-nat (bv)\n  (if (endp bv)\n      0\n    (+ (if (car bv) 1 0)\n       (* 2 (bv-to-nat (cdr bv))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Convert a natural number to a bit vector of width w\n(defun nat-to-bv (n w)\n  (if (zp w)\n      nil\n    (cons (not (equal (mod n 2) 0))\n          (nat-to-bv (floor n 2) (- w 1)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test conversions\n(list (bv-to-nat (list t t nil nil))   ; 3\n      (bv-to-nat (list t nil t nil))   ; 5\n      (nat-to-bv 13 4))                ; (t nil t t) = 13"))

cells.append(code("; Round-trip property: converting a small number to bv and back\n(thm\n  (implies (and (natp n)\n                (natp w)\n                (< n (expt 2 w)))\n           (equal (bv-to-nat (nat-to-bv n w)) n)))"))

cells.append(md("### Bit-Vector Addition\n\nWe define addition on bit vectors using our ripple-carry adder:"))

cells.append(code("; Bit-vector addition\n(defun bv-add (x y)\n  (ripple-carry-adder x y nil))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: 3 + 5 = 8\n(bv-to-nat (bv-add (nat-to-bv 3 4)\n                   (nat-to-bv 5 4)))"))

cells.append(md("### Correctness of Bit-Vector Addition\n\nThe key correctness theorem for our adder states that bit-vector addition correctly implements modular arithmetic:\n\n$$\\text{bv-to-nat}(\\text{bv-add}(x, y)) = (\\text{bv-to-nat}(x) + \\text{bv-to-nat}(y))$$\n\nNote that `bv-add` may produce an output one bit wider than the inputs (due to carry-out), so the full sum is captured without modular truncation."))

cells.append(code("; Adder correctness: the adder computes the arithmetic sum.\n; (The ripple-carry adder preserves the full sum including carry.)\n(defthm ripple-carry-adder-correct\n  (equal (bv-to-nat (ripple-carry-adder x y cin))\n         (+ (bv-to-nat x)\n            (bv-to-nat y)\n            (bool-to-nat cin))))"))

# 5. SEQUENTIAL CIRCUITS
cells.append(md("## 7.5 Sequential Circuits\n\nUnlike combinational circuits, **sequential circuits** have memory — their outputs depend on both current inputs and stored state. The fundamental storage element is the **flip-flop**, which captures a value on each clock tick.\n\nWe model sequential circuits by making state explicit: a circuit is a function from `(state, input) → (new-state, output)`."))

cells.append(code("; A simple 4-bit counter circuit.\n; State is a 4-bit number (0..15), input is an enable signal.\n; When enabled, the counter increments (mod 16).\n(defun counter-next (state enable)\n  (if enable\n      (mod (+ state 1) 16)\n    state))"))

cells.append(code("; Run the counter for n clock cycles, all enabled\n(defun run-counter (state n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      state\n    (run-counter (counter-next state t) (- n 1))))"))

cells.append(code("; The counter counts correctly: after n steps from 0,\n; the state is n mod 16.\n(defthm counter-correct\n  (implies (and (natp n)\n                (natp state)\n                (< state 16))\n           (equal (run-counter state n)\n                  (mod (+ state n) 16))))"))

cells.append(md("### State Machine Example\n\nLet's model a simple state machine — a traffic light controller with states `green`, `yellow`, and `red`:"))

cells.append(code("; Traffic light state machine\n(defun next-light (state)\n  (cond ((equal state 'green)  'yellow)\n        ((equal state 'yellow) 'red)\n        ((equal state 'red)    'green)\n        (t                     'red)))  ; default"))

cells.append(code("; Run the traffic light for n cycles\n(defun run-light (state n)\n  (if (zp n)\n      state\n    (run-light (next-light state) (- n 1))))"))

cells.append(code("; After 3 cycles, we return to the same state\n(thm\n  (implies (member-equal state '(green yellow red))\n           (equal (run-light state 3) state)))"))

# 6. FM9001
cells.append(md("## 7.6 The FM9001 Story\n\nThe **FM9001** is a 32-bit microprocessor whose gate-level design was **fully verified** in ACL2 by Warren A. Hunt, Jr. This is one of the landmark achievements in formal verification.\n\n### What Was Proved\n\nThe FM9001 verification established that:\n\n- The **gate-level netlist** (the actual hardware design) correctly implements\n- A **high-level instruction set specification**\n- For **all possible inputs and initial states**\n\nThis means every instruction, every addressing mode, and every edge case was formally verified — not tested, but *proved*.\n\n### Scale of the Proof\n\n| Aspect | Detail |\n|--------|--------|\n| Processor | 32-bit, custom ISA |\n| Gate count | ~10,000 gates |\n| Lines of ACL2 | ~20,000 |\n| Properties proved | Complete functional correctness |\n| Years of effort | ~3 years |\n\n### Where to Find It\n\nThe FM9001 proof is available in the ACL2 Community Books:\n\n```\nbooks/projects/fm9001/\n```\n\nKey files include the netlist description, the ISA specification, and the main correctness theorems linking them."))

cells.append(md("### Significance\n\nThe FM9001 demonstrated that:\n\n1. **Formal verification of processors is feasible** — you can prove a real processor correct\n2. **The ACL2 approach scales** — from single gates to complete processors\n3. **Hierarchical verification works** — verify gates → modules → processor\n\nThis work influenced industrial practice: similar techniques are now used at AMD, Intel, ARM, and other chip companies for verifying critical components.\n\nOther notable ACL2 hardware verification projects include:\n- **Centaur Technology** (now AMD): Verified floating-point units in production processors\n- **VIA processor verification**: Using ACL2 for commercial x86 verification\n- The `books/centaur/` directory contains many industrially-relevant hardware verification tools"))

cells.append(md("## 7.7 Exercises"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 7.1: Define OR using only NAND gates\n; Hint: OR(a,b) = NAND(NOT(a), NOT(b)) = NAND(NAND(a,a), NAND(b,b))\n\n(defun b-or-from-nand (a b)\n  ;; YOUR DEFINITION HERE\n  nil)\n\n; Prove equivalence:\n; (thm (equal (b-or-from-nand a b) (b-or a b)))"))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. INTRODUCTION
cells.append(md("# Module 8: SAT Solving and Boolean Reasoning\n\n**ACL2 for Computer Science — University-Level Tutorial Series**"))

cells.append(md("## Learning Objectives\n\nBy the end of this module you will be able to:\n\n1. Represent boolean formulas in conjunctive normal form (CNF)\n2. Evaluate CNF formulas against variable assignments\n3. Implement truth-table–based tautology checking\n4. Understand and implement unit propagation\n5. Build a simple DPLL SAT solver in ACL2 and prove its soundness\n6. Know the ACL2 community books related to SAT and BDDs"))

cells.append(md("## 8.1 Introduction\n\n**Boolean satisfiability (SAT)** is the canonical NP-complete problem: given a boolean formula, is there an assignment of truth values to its variables that makes the formula true?\n\nSAT solving is central to:\n- **Hardware verification** (checking circuit equivalence)\n- **Software verification** (bounded model checking)\n- **AI planning** and **scheduling**\n- **Cryptanalysis**\n\nACL2 has extensive SAT-related books and tools, including verified SAT proof checkers that can validate proofs produced by industrial SAT solvers."))

cells.append(md("### The Landscape of SAT in ACL2\n\n| Tool / Book | Purpose |\n|---|---|\n| `books/clause-processors/` | Integration of external SAT solvers |\n| `books/projects/sat/` | SAT solver implementations and proofs |\n| `books/centaur/satlink/` | Connecting ACL2 to external SAT solvers |\n| `books/projects/sat/lrat/` | Verified LRAT proof checker |\n| ACL2's built-in BDDs | Binary Decision Diagram support |"))

# 2. BOOLEAN FORMULAS
cells.append(md("## 8.2 Boolean Formulas in CNF\n\nWe represent formulas in **Conjunctive Normal Form (CNF)** — the standard input format for SAT solvers.\n\n**Conventions:**\n- A **literal** is a non-zero integer: positive means the variable is true, negative means it is negated. E.g., `3` means $x_3$ and `-3` means $\\neg x_3$.\n- A **clause** is a list of literals representing their disjunction (OR).\n- A **formula** is a list of clauses representing their conjunction (AND).\n\nFor example, the formula $(x_1 \\vee \\neg x_2) \\wedge (x_2 \\vee x_3)$ is represented as `((1 -2) (2 3))`."))

cells.append(md("### Variable Assignments\n\nAn **assignment** is an association list mapping variable numbers (positive integers) to booleans:"))

cells.append(code("; Look up a variable's value in an assignment.\n; Unassigned variables default to nil.\n(defun lookup-var (var assignment)\n  (cond ((endp assignment) nil)\n        ((equal (car (car assignment)) var)\n         (cdr (car assignment)))\n        (t (lookup-var var (cdr assignment)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test assignment: x1=t, x2=nil, x3=t\n(defconst *test-assignment*\n  '((1 . t) (2 . nil) (3 . t)))"))

cells.append(code("; Look up some values\n(list (lookup-var 1 *test-assignment*)   ; t\n      (lookup-var 2 *test-assignment*)   ; nil\n      (lookup-var 3 *test-assignment*)   ; t\n      (lookup-var 4 *test-assignment*))  ; nil (default)"))

cells.append(md("### Evaluating Formulas"))

cells.append(code("; Evaluate a literal under an assignment.\n; A positive literal n is true iff variable n is true.\n; A negative literal -n is true iff variable n is false.\n(defun eval-literal (lit assignment)\n  (if (< lit 0)\n      (not (lookup-var (- lit) assignment))\n    (lookup-var lit assignment)))"))

cells.append(code("; Evaluate a clause (disjunction of literals).\n; A clause is true iff at least one literal is true.\n(defun eval-clause (clause assignment)\n  (if (endp clause)\n      nil\n    (if (eval-literal (car clause) assignment)\n        t\n      (eval-clause (cdr clause) assignment))))"))

cells.append(code("; Evaluate a formula (conjunction of clauses).\n; A formula is true iff every clause is true.\n(defun eval-formula (formula assignment)\n  (if (endp formula)\n      t\n    (if (eval-clause (car formula) assignment)\n        (eval-formula (cdr formula) assignment)\n      nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: (x1 OR NOT x2) AND (x2 OR x3)\n; with x1=t, x2=nil, x3=t\n(eval-formula '((1 -2) (2 3)) *test-assignment*)\n; clause 1: x1=t so true; clause 2: x2=nil but x3=t so true → t"))

cells.append(code("; Test: an unsatisfied formula under this assignment\n; (NOT x1) AND (x1)  → always unsatisfiable\n(eval-formula '((-1) (1)) *test-assignment*)\n; clause 1: NOT x1 = nil → formula is nil"))

# 3. TRUTH TABLES AND TAUTOLOGY CHECKING
cells.append(md("## 8.3 Truth Tables and Tautology Checking\n\nFor formulas with a small number of variables, we can check all possible assignments. This gives us a **truth-table** approach to tautology checking.\n\nA **tautology** is a formula that is true under *every* assignment."))

cells.append(code("; Generate all possible assignments for variables 1..n.\n; Returns a list of assignments.\n(defun all-assignments-helper (n base)\n  (if (zp n)\n      (list base)\n    (append (all-assignments-helper (- n 1)\n                                     (cons (cons n nil) base))\n            (all-assignments-helper (- n 1)\n                                     (cons (cons n t) base)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Top-level: generate all 2^n assignments for n variables\n(defun all-assignments (n)\n  (all-assignments-helper n nil))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: all assignments for 2 variables\n(all-assignments 2)"))

cells.append(code("; Check if a formula is a tautology:\n; true under every assignment for variables 1..n\n(defun tautologyp (formula n)\n  (tautologyp-helper formula (all-assignments n)))\n\n(defun tautologyp-helper (formula assignments)\n  (if (endp assignments)\n      t\n    (if (eval-formula formula (car assignments))\n        (tautologyp-helper formula (cdr assignments))\n      nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; Tautology: p OR NOT p → ((1) (-1)) is NOT a tautology in CNF!\n; In CNF, ((1) (-1)) means (x1) AND (NOT x1) which is UNSAT.\n; The tautology p OR NOT p is a single clause: ((1 -1))\n(tautologyp '((1 -1)) 1)"))

cells.append(code("; ((1 -1)) is indeed a tautology (the clause x1 OR NOT x1)\n; ((1) (-1)) is unsatisfiable (x1 AND NOT x1)\n(list (tautologyp '((1 -1)) 1)      ; t (tautology)\n      (tautologyp '((1) (-1)) 1))    ; nil (not a tautology)"))

cells.append(md("The truth-table approach works but is exponential in the number of variables ($2^n$ assignments). For practical formulas with thousands of variables, we need smarter algorithms."))

# 4. UNIT PROPAGATION
cells.append(md("## 8.4 Unit Propagation\n\nUnit propagation is a core technique in modern SAT solvers. The idea is simple:\n\n> If a clause has only **one unassigned literal**, that literal **must be true** for the clause to be satisfied.\n\nThis forced assignment may simplify other clauses, triggering further propagations."))

cells.append(code("; Check if a clause is a unit clause under an assignment.\n; A unit clause has exactly one unassigned literal and\n; all other literals evaluate to false.\n(defun find-unit-literal (clause assignment)\n  (find-unit-literal-helper clause assignment nil))\n\n(defun find-unit-literal-helper (clause assignment unassigned-lit)\n  (if (endp clause)\n      unassigned-lit  ; returns nil if 0 unassigned, the literal if exactly 1\n    (let ((lit (car clause)))\n      (if (eval-literal lit assignment)\n          nil  ; clause already satisfied\n        (let ((var (if (< lit 0) (- lit) lit)))\n          (if (lookup-var var assignment)\n              ;; variable is assigned but literal is false, skip\n              (find-unit-literal-helper (cdr clause) assignment unassigned-lit)\n            ;; variable is unassigned\n            (if unassigned-lit\n                nil  ; more than one unassigned → not unit\n              (find-unit-literal-helper (cdr clause) assignment lit))))))))"))

cells.append(md("### Performing Unit Propagation\n\nWhen we find a unit literal, we add the forced assignment and repeat:"))

cells.append(code("; Assign a literal: if lit > 0, set var to t; if lit < 0, set var to nil\n(defun assign-literal (lit assignment)\n  (if (< lit 0)\n      (cons (cons (- lit) nil) assignment)\n    (cons (cons lit t) assignment)))"))

cells.append(code("; Find any unit clause in a formula and return its forced literal,\n; or nil if no unit clause exists.\n(defun find-unit-in-formula (formula assignment)\n  (if (endp formula)\n      nil\n    (let ((unit-lit (find-unit-literal (car formula) assignment)))\n      (if unit-lit\n          unit-lit\n        (find-unit-in-formula (cdr formula) assignment)))))"))

cells.append(code("; Perform unit propagation: repeatedly find and assign unit literals.\n; Returns the extended assignment.\n; Uses a step counter to ensure termination.\n(defun unit-propagate (formula assignment steps)\n  (if (zp steps)\n      assignment\n    (let ((unit-lit (find-unit-in-formula formula assignment)))\n      (if unit-lit\n          (unit-propagate formula\n                         (assign-literal unit-lit assignment)\n                         (- steps 1))\n        assignment))))"))

cells.append(md("### Soundness of Unit Propagation\n\nThe key property of unit propagation is that it **preserves satisfiability**: if the original formula is satisfiable, it remains satisfiable after forced assignments.\n\nFormally, any satisfying assignment must agree with the forced assignments (otherwise a unit clause would be violated)."))

cells.append(code("; Test unit propagation on ((1) (-1 2) (-2 3))\n; Clause (1) forces x1=t.\n; Then (-1 2) becomes a unit: since x1=t, -1 is false, so 2 is forced → x2=t.\n; Then (-2 3) becomes a unit: since x2=t, -2 is false, so 3 is forced → x3=t.\n(unit-propagate '((1) (-1 2) (-2 3)) nil 10)"))

# 5. DPLL SOLVER
cells.append(md("## 8.5 A Simple DPLL Solver\n\nThe **Davis–Putnam–Logemann–Loveland (DPLL)** algorithm is the foundation of all modern SAT solvers. It combines:\n\n1. **Unit propagation** to simplify the formula\n2. **Branching** — pick an unassigned variable, try both true and false\n3. **Backtracking** — if one branch fails, try the other\n\nDPLL returns either a satisfying assignment or `'UNSAT`."))

cells.append(code("; Check if a formula is satisfied by an assignment\n(defun formula-satisfied-p (formula assignment)\n  (eval-formula formula assignment))"))

cells.append(code("; Check if any clause is falsified (all its literals are false\n; under the current assignment, with all variables assigned)\n(defun clause-falsified-p (clause assignment)\n  (if (endp clause)\n      t  ; empty clause is falsified\n    (if (eval-literal (car clause) assignment)\n        nil  ; found a true literal\n      (let ((var (if (< (car clause) 0) (- (car clause)) (car clause))))\n        (if (lookup-var var assignment)\n            (clause-falsified-p (cdr clause) assignment)\n          nil)))))  ; unassigned variable → not yet falsified"))

cells.append(code("; Check if any clause in the formula is falsified\n(defun has-falsified-clause (formula assignment)\n  (if (endp formula)\n      nil\n    (if (clause-falsified-p (car formula) assignment)\n        t\n      (has-falsified-clause (cdr formula) assignment))))"))

cells.append(code("; Pick the first unassigned variable from a formula.\n; Scans all literals to find a variable not in the assignment.\n(defun pick-variable (formula assignment)\n  (pick-variable-from-clauses formula assignment))\n\n(defun pick-variable-from-clauses (formula assignment)\n  (if (endp formula)\n      nil\n    (let ((v (pick-variable-from-clause (car formula) assignment)))\n      (if v v\n        (pick-variable-from-clauses (cdr formula) assignment)))))\n\n(defun pick-variable-from-clause (clause assignment)\n  (if (endp clause)\n      nil\n    (let* ((lit (car clause))\n           (var (if (< lit 0) (- lit) lit)))\n      (if (lookup-var var assignment)\n          (pick-variable-from-clause (cdr clause) assignment)\n        var))))"))

cells.append(code("; The DPLL SAT solver.\n; Uses a step counter for termination.\n(defun dpll (formula assignment steps)\n  (if (zp steps)\n      'UNKNOWN\n    (let ((assignment (unit-propagate formula assignment 100)))\n      (cond\n       ;; Check if formula is satisfied\n       ((formula-satisfied-p formula assignment)\n        assignment)\n       ;; Check if any clause is falsified → backtrack\n       ((has-falsified-clause formula assignment)\n        'UNSAT)\n       ;; Branch on an unassigned variable\n       (t\n        (let ((var (pick-variable formula assignment)))\n          (if (not var)\n              ;; No unassigned variables left\n              (if (formula-satisfied-p formula assignment)\n                  assignment\n                'UNSAT)\n            ;; Try var = t first\n            (let ((result (dpll formula\n                               (cons (cons var t) assignment)\n                               (- steps 1))))\n              (if (not (equal result 'UNSAT))\n                  result\n                ;; Try var = nil\n                (dpll formula\n                      (cons (cons var nil) assignment)\n                      (- steps 1)))))))))))"))

cells.append(md("### Testing the DPLL Solver"))

cells.append(code("; Satisfiable: (x1 OR x2) AND (NOT x1 OR x3) AND (NOT x3 OR x2)\n(dpll '((1 2) (-1 3) (-3 2)) nil 100)"))

cells.append(code("; Unsatisfiable: (x1) AND (NOT x1)\n(dpll '((1) (-1)) nil 100)"))

cells.append(code("; A more complex satisfiable formula:\n; (x1 OR x2) AND (NOT x1 OR NOT x2) AND (x1 OR NOT x2) \n; Satisfiable with x1=t, x2=nil\n(dpll '((1 2) (-1 -2) (1 -2)) nil 100)"))

cells.append(md("### Soundness of DPLL\n\nThe key soundness property is: **if DPLL returns an assignment, that assignment satisfies the formula.**"))

cells.append(code("; Verify the answer: check that the returned assignment\n; actually satisfies the formula.\n(let ((result (dpll '((1 2) (-1 3) (-3 2)) nil 100)))\n  (if (not (equal result 'UNSAT))\n      (eval-formula '((1 2) (-1 3) (-3 2)) result)\n    'was-unsat))"))

# 6. SAT IN ACL2 COMMUNITY BOOKS
cells.append(md("## 8.6 SAT in the ACL2 Community Books\n\nThe ACL2 community has developed industrial-strength SAT tools:\n\n### Verified SAT Proof Checkers\n\nModern SAT solvers can emit **proof certificates** (LRAT/DRAT format) that attest to unsatisfiability. ACL2's verified checkers can validate these certificates with mathematical certainty:\n\n- **`books/projects/sat/lrat/`** — A verified LRAT (Linear Resolution Asymmetric Tautology) proof checker\n- The checker itself is proved correct in ACL2: if it accepts a proof, the formula is truly unsatisfiable\n- This gives a **verified pipeline**: untrusted SAT solver → proof → verified checker\n\n### SATLink\n\n**`books/centaur/satlink/`** provides an interface between ACL2 and external SAT solvers (like Glucose, CryptoMiniSat, etc.). It:\n\n1. Translates ACL2 boolean formulas to DIMACS CNF format\n2. Calls an external solver\n3. Validates the result using a verified proof checker\n\n### SULFA\n\n**`books/clause-processors/SULFA/`** provides a Subclass of Unrollable List Formulas in ACL2 — a way to reduce ACL2 conjectures to SAT problems."))

# 7. BDDs
cells.append(md("## 8.7 BDDs (Binary Decision Diagrams)\n\nA **Binary Decision Diagram (BDD)** is a compact data structure for representing boolean functions. Unlike CNF, BDDs support efficient equivalence checking — two BDDs represent the same function if and only if they are structurally equal.\n\nACL2 has built-in support for BDD-based reasoning through its `IF`-then-else normal form."))

cells.append(md("### How BDDs Work\n\nA BDD is a directed acyclic graph where:\n- Each internal node is a variable that branches on true/false\n- Leaf nodes are `T` or `NIL`\n- Variables appear in a fixed order on all paths\n- Isomorphic sub-graphs are shared (canonicity)\n\nFor a function $f(x_1, x_2, x_3)$, the BDD encodes the **Shannon expansion**:\n\n$$f = (x_1 \\wedge f|_{x_1=1}) \\vee (\\neg x_1 \\wedge f|_{x_1=0})$$"))

cells.append(code("; ACL2 can reason about IF-then-else trees directly.\n; This is related to BDD reasoning.\n; Example: verify that two IF-expressions compute the same function.\n(thm\n  (equal (if a (if b t nil) nil)\n         (if b (if a t nil) nil)))"))

cells.append(code("; A more complex equivalence:\n; (a AND b) OR (a AND NOT b) = a\n(thm\n  (equal (if (if a (if b t nil) nil)\n             t\n           (if (if a (if b nil t) nil)\n               t\n             nil))\n         (if a t nil)))"))

cells.append(md("### BDDs in the Community Books\n\nThe **`books/centaur/ubdds/`** library provides a uniquified BDD package for ACL2 with:\n\n- Efficient BDD construction and manipulation\n- Verified canonicity properties\n- Integration with hardware verification tools\n\nBDDs complement SAT solving: BDDs work well for equivalence checking of moderate-size circuits, while SAT excels at checking constraints on large circuits."))

# 8. EXERCISES
cells.append(md("## 8.8 Exercises"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 8.1: Formula Construction\n\nEncode the following formula in CNF and check it with `eval-formula`:\n\n$$(x_1 \\Rightarrow x_2) \\wedge (x_2 \\Rightarrow x_3) \\wedge x_1 \\wedge \\neg x_3$$\n\nRecall that $A \\Rightarrow B$ is equivalent to $\\neg A \\vee B$. Is this formula satisfiable?"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 8.1: Encode and check the formula\n; (x1 => x2) AND (x2 => x3) AND x1 AND NOT x3\n;\n; Hint: x1 => x2 becomes clause (-1 2)\n;\n; YOUR CODE HERE\n; (dpll '(...) nil 100)"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 8.2: Resolution\n\nThe **resolution rule** is: from clauses $(A \\vee x)$ and $(B \\vee \\neg x)$, derive $(A \\vee B)$.\n\nDefine a function `resolve` that takes two clauses and a variable, and returns the resolvent. Then verify that if both input clauses are satisfied, the resolvent is also satisfied."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 8.2: Resolution\n;\n; (defun resolve (clause1 clause2 var)\n;   ;; Remove +var from clause1, -var from clause2,\n;   ;; return the union of remaining literals\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; Test: resolve '(1 2) '(-1 3) 1 → (2 3)"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 8.3: Pure Literal Elimination\n\nA literal is **pure** in a formula if it appears only positively (or only negatively). Pure literals can be assigned to make their clauses true.\n\nDefine `find-pure-literal` that scans a formula and returns a pure literal if one exists."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 8.3: Pure literal elimination\n;\n; (defun find-pure-literal (formula)\n;   ;; Scan all literals. If a variable appears only positive\n;   ;; or only negative, return that literal.\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; Test: in ((1 2) (1 3) (-2 3)), literal 1 is pure (always positive)\n; (find-pure-literal '((1 2) (1 3) (-2 3)))  → 1 or 3"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 8.4: Counting Solutions\n\nDefine `count-solutions` that counts how many assignments (out of all $2^n$) satisfy a given formula. Use the `all-assignments` function."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 8.4: Count solutions\n;\n; (defun count-solutions (formula n)\n;   ;; Count how many of the 2^n assignments satisfy formula\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; Test: (1 -1) is a tautology over 1 variable → 2 solutions\n; (count-solutions '((1 -1)) 1) → 2\n;\n; (1) AND (-1) over 1 variable → 0 solutions\n; (count-solutions '((1) (-1)) 1) → 0"))

import json, uuid

def md(src):
    return {"cell_type": "markdown", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

def code(src):
    return {"cell_type": "code", "metadata": {}, "source": [src], "outputs": [], "execution_count": None, "id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8]}

cells = []

# 1. INTRODUCTION
cells.append(md("# Module 9: Cryptography and Security\n\n**ACL2 for Computer Science — University-Level Tutorial Series**"))

cells.append(md("## Learning Objectives\n\nBy the end of this module you will be able to:\n\n1. Implement and verify modular arithmetic operations in ACL2\n2. Understand the mathematical foundations of RSA\n3. Model and reason about hash functions formally\n4. Appreciate how ACL2 has been used to verify real cryptographic implementations\n5. Connect formal verification to security guarantees"))

cells.append(md("## 9.1 Introduction — Why Formal Verification for Cryptography?\n\nCryptographic algorithms are among the most security-critical software in existence. A single bug can compromise the confidentiality of millions of communications.\n\nFormal verification matters for cryptography because:\n\n- **Subtle bugs are devastating**: Off-by-one errors or incorrect modular reductions can completely break security\n- **Testing is insufficient**: Crypto bugs often produce outputs that *look* random and correct but fail against sophisticated attacks\n- **Correctness is mathematical**: Crypto algorithms are precisely specified by mathematical definitions — ideal for theorem proving\n\nACL2 has been used to verify several real cryptographic implementations, including SHA-256, AES, and elliptic curve operations."))

cells.append(md("### ACL2 Crypto Verification Projects\n\n| Project | Location | What Was Verified |\n|---------|----------|-------------------|\n| SHA-2 | `books/projects/sha-2/` | SHA-256 and SHA-512 hash functions |\n| AES | `books/projects/aes/` | AES block cipher |\n| Elliptic Curves | `books/kestrel/crypto/ecurve/` | Elliptic curve operations |\n| HMAC | `books/kestrel/crypto/hmac/` | HMAC construction |\n| x86 Crypto | `books/projects/x86isa/` | x86 crypto instruction models |"))

# 2. MODULAR ARITHMETIC
cells.append(md("## 9.2 Modular Arithmetic\n\nModular arithmetic is the foundation of nearly all public-key cryptography. We work with integers modulo some number $n$, where all operations \"wrap around\" at $n$.\n\nKey identity:\n\n$$a \\cdot b \\pmod{n} = ((a \\bmod n) \\cdot (b \\bmod n)) \\pmod{n}$$\n\nThis property is essential for efficient modular exponentiation."))

cells.append(code("; Basic modular arithmetic in ACL2.\n; ACL2's built-in mod function handles this.\n(list (mod 17 5)       ; 2\n      (mod 100 7)      ; 2\n      (mod (* 3 4) 5)  ; 2\n      (mod 0 5))       ; 0"))

cells.append(code("; The fundamental property of modular multiplication:\n; (mod (* a b) n) = (mod (* (mod a n) (mod b n)) n)\n(thm\n  (implies (and (natp a) (natp b)\n                (posp n))\n           (equal (mod (* (mod a n) (mod b n)) n)\n                  (mod (* a b) n))))"))

cells.append(md("### Modular Exponentiation by Repeated Squaring\n\nComputing $a^e \\pmod{n}$ by multiplying $a$ by itself $e$ times would be hopelessly slow for cryptographic-size numbers (e.g., $e > 2^{256}$).\n\n**Repeated squaring** computes this in $O(\\log e)$ multiplications using the identity:\n\n$$a^e = \\begin{cases} 1 & \\text{if } e = 0 \\\\ (a^{e/2})^2 & \\text{if } e \\text{ is even} \\\\ a \\cdot a^{e-1} & \\text{if } e \\text{ is odd} \\end{cases}$$"))

cells.append(code("; Modular exponentiation by repeated squaring\n(defun mod-exp (base exp modulus)\n  (declare (xargs :guard (and (natp base)\n                              (natp exp)\n                              (posp modulus))))\n  (cond ((zp exp) (mod 1 modulus))\n        ((evenp exp)\n         (let ((half (mod-exp base (/ exp 2) modulus)))\n           (mod (* half half) modulus)))\n        (t\n         (mod (* base (mod-exp base (- exp 1) modulus))\n              modulus))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: 2^10 mod 1000 = 1024 mod 1000 = 24\n(mod-exp 2 10 1000)"))

cells.append(code("; Test: 3^13 mod 7\n; 3^13 = 1594323, mod 7 = 3\n(mod-exp 3 13 7)"))

cells.append(code("; Verify against direct computation\n(equal (mod-exp 5 117 19)\n       (mod (expt 5 117) 19))"))

cells.append(md("### The Euclidean Algorithm (GCD)\n\nThe **greatest common divisor** is computed efficiently by the Euclidean algorithm and is fundamental to RSA key generation."))

cells.append(code("; Euclidean algorithm for GCD\n(defun gcd-fn (a b)\n  (declare (xargs :guard (and (natp a) (natp b))))\n  (if (zp b)\n      a\n    (gcd-fn b (mod a b))))"))

cells.append(code("; Tests\n(list (gcd-fn 12 8)    ; 4\n      (gcd-fn 35 15)   ; 5\n      (gcd-fn 17 13)   ; 1 (coprime)\n      (gcd-fn 100 0))  ; 100"))

cells.append(code("; GCD divides both arguments\n(thm\n  (implies (and (natp a) (posp b))\n           (equal (mod a (gcd-fn a b))\n                  0))\n  :hints ((\"Goal\" :in-theory (enable gcd-fn))))"))

cells.append(code("; The GCD divides b as well\n(thm\n  (implies (and (natp a) (posp b))\n           (equal (mod b (gcd-fn a b))\n                  0))\n  :hints ((\"Goal\" :in-theory (enable gcd-fn))))"))

cells.append(md("### Extended Euclidean Algorithm\n\nFor RSA, we need the **modular inverse**: given $e$ and $\\phi(n)$, find $d$ such that $e \\cdot d \\equiv 1 \\pmod{\\phi(n)}$.\n\nThe extended Euclidean algorithm finds $x, y$ such that $ax + by = \\gcd(a, b)$."))

cells.append(code("; Extended Euclidean algorithm\n; Returns (list gcd x y) such that a*x + b*y = gcd\n(defun ext-gcd (a b)\n  (if (zp b)\n      (list a 1 0)\n    (let* ((result (ext-gcd b (mod a b)))\n           (g (first result))\n           (x (second result))\n           (y (third result)))\n      (list g y (- x (* (floor a b) y))))))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: gcd(35, 15) = 5, and we find x, y such that 35x + 15y = 5\n(let ((result (ext-gcd 35 15)))\n  (list result\n        (+ (* 35 (second result))\n           (* 15 (third result)))))  ; should equal 5"))

cells.append(code("; Modular inverse: find d such that e*d ≡ 1 (mod n)\n(defun mod-inverse (e n)\n  (let* ((result (ext-gcd e n))\n         (d (second result)))\n    (mod d n)))"))

cells.append(code("; Test: inverse of 3 mod 11\n; 3 * 4 = 12 ≡ 1 (mod 11)\n(mod-inverse 3 11)"))

# 3. RSA FOUNDATIONS
cells.append(md("## 9.3 RSA Foundations\n\nThe **RSA** cryptosystem (Rivest–Shamir–Adleman) is one of the most widely used public-key algorithms. Its security rests on the difficulty of factoring large numbers.\n\n### Key Generation\n\n1. Choose two large primes $p$ and $q$\n2. Compute $n = p \\cdot q$\n3. Compute $\\phi(n) = (p-1)(q-1)$\n4. Choose $e$ coprime to $\\phi(n)$ (typically $e = 65537$)\n5. Compute $d = e^{-1} \\pmod{\\phi(n)}$\n\n**Public key**: $(n, e)$ — **Private key**: $(n, d)$\n\n### Encryption and Decryption\n\n$$\\text{encrypt}(m) = m^e \\pmod{n}$$\n$$\\text{decrypt}(c) = c^d \\pmod{n}$$\n\n**RSA Correctness Theorem**: $m^{ed} \\equiv m \\pmod{n}$ for all $0 \\le m < n$.\n\nThis follows from Euler's theorem: $a^{\\phi(n)} \\equiv 1 \\pmod{n}$ when $\\gcd(a, n) = 1$."))

cells.append(code("; RSA encryption\n(defun rsa-encrypt (message e n)\n  (mod-exp message e n))"))

cells.append(code("; RSA decryption\n(defun rsa-decrypt (ciphertext d n)\n  (mod-exp ciphertext d n))"))

cells.append(md("### A Small RSA Example\n\nLet's work through RSA with small primes to see the algorithm in action:"))

cells.append(code("; RSA with small primes for demonstration\n; p = 61, q = 53\n(let* ((p 61)\n       (q 53)\n       (n (* p q))          ; 3233\n       (phi (* (- p 1) (- q 1)))  ; 3120\n       (e 17)               ; coprime to 3120\n       (d (mod-inverse e phi)))  ; d such that 17*d ≡ 1 (mod 3120)\n  (list :n n\n        :phi phi\n        :e e\n        :d d\n        :check-ed-mod-phi (mod (* e d) phi)))  ; should be 1"))

cells.append(code("; Encrypt and decrypt a message\n(let* ((p 61)\n       (q 53)\n       (n (* p q))\n       (phi (* (- p 1) (- q 1)))\n       (e 17)\n       (d (mod-inverse e phi))\n       (message 42)\n       (ciphertext (rsa-encrypt message e n))\n       (decrypted  (rsa-decrypt ciphertext d n)))\n  (list :message message\n        :ciphertext ciphertext\n        :decrypted decrypted\n        :correct (equal decrypted message)))"))

cells.append(md("### The RSA Correctness Theorem\n\nThe full proof of RSA correctness requires **Fermat's Little Theorem** (or more generally, Euler's theorem):\n\n$$a^{p-1} \\equiv 1 \\pmod{p} \\quad \\text{when } \\gcd(a, p) = 1$$\n\nFrom this, one derives that $m^{ed} = m^{1 + k\\phi(n)} = m \\cdot (m^{\\phi(n)})^k \\equiv m \\cdot 1^k = m \\pmod{n}$.\n\nA full formal proof of this theorem in ACL2 requires significant number theory infrastructure. The `books/projects/numbers/` directory contains relevant foundations."))

# 4. HASH FUNCTIONS
cells.append(md("## 9.4 Hash Functions\n\nA **cryptographic hash function** maps arbitrary-length input to a fixed-length output (the **digest**) with these properties:\n\n1. **Deterministic**: Same input always gives same output\n2. **One-way**: Given $h(m)$, it is infeasible to find $m$\n3. **Collision-resistant**: Hard to find $m_1 \\neq m_2$ with $h(m_1) = h(m_2)$\n4. **Avalanche effect**: Small input changes cause large output changes"))

cells.append(code("; A simple (non-cryptographic!) hash function over lists.\n; This is for illustration only — not secure!\n(defun simple-hash (lst modulus)\n  (if (endp lst)\n      0\n    (mod (+ (* 31 (simple-hash (cdr lst) modulus))\n            (if (natp (car lst)) (car lst) 0))\n         modulus)))"))

cells.append(code("; Test our hash function\n(list (simple-hash '(72 101 108 108 111) 1000000)   ; \"Hello\"\n      (simple-hash '(72 101 108 108 112) 1000000)   ; \"Hellp\" (different)\n      (simple-hash '(72 101 108 108 111) 1000000))  ; Same as first (deterministic)"))

cells.append(code("; Prove determinism: same input → same output\n(thm\n  (equal (simple-hash lst modulus)\n         (simple-hash lst modulus)))"))

cells.append(code("; A stronger determinism property:\n; if two lists are equal, their hashes are equal\n(thm\n  (implies (equal lst1 lst2)\n           (equal (simple-hash lst1 modulus)\n                  (simple-hash lst2 modulus))))"))

cells.append(code("; Our hash always produces a result in [0, modulus)\n(thm\n  (implies (posp modulus)\n           (and (natp (simple-hash lst modulus))\n                (< (simple-hash lst modulus) modulus))))"))

cells.append(md("### Real Hash Verification in ACL2\n\nThe `books/projects/sha-2/` library contains a formal model of **SHA-256** and **SHA-512** — the hash functions used in TLS, Bitcoin, digital signatures, and countless other applications.\n\nThe verification establishes that:\n- The ACL2 implementation matches the NIST specification exactly\n- The implementation handles all edge cases (padding, length encoding)\n- Properties like output size are formally proved"))

# 5. MESSAGE AUTHENTICATION
cells.append(md("## 9.5 Message Authentication\n\nA **Message Authentication Code (MAC)** allows a recipient to verify both the integrity and authenticity of a message.\n\n### HMAC\n\n**HMAC** (Hash-based MAC) is the standard construction:\n\n$$\\text{HMAC}(K, m) = H((K' \\oplus \\text{opad}) \\| H((K' \\oplus \\text{ipad}) \\| m))$$\n\nwhere $K'$ is the key (padded to block size), and ipad/opad are fixed constants."))

cells.append(code("; A simplified MAC scheme for illustration.\n; MAC(key, message) = hash(key ++ message)\n; (Real HMAC uses the nested construction above)\n(defun simple-mac (key message modulus)\n  (simple-hash (append key message) modulus))"))

cells.append(code("; Verify a MAC\n(defun verify-mac (key message expected-mac modulus)\n  (equal (simple-mac key message modulus)\n         expected-mac))"))

cells.append(code("; Test MAC generation and verification\n(let* ((key '(1 2 3))\n       (msg '(72 101 108 108 111))\n       (mac (simple-mac key msg 1000000)))\n  (list :mac mac\n        :verify-correct (verify-mac key msg mac 1000000)\n        :verify-tampered (verify-mac key '(72 101 108 108 112) mac 1000000)))"))

cells.append(code("; Correctness: verification always succeeds with the right MAC\n(thm\n  (verify-mac key message\n              (simple-mac key message modulus)\n              modulus))"))

cells.append(md("### HMAC in ACL2\n\nThe `books/kestrel/crypto/hmac/` library contains formal models of HMAC, verified against the RFC specification."))

# 6. VERIFIED CRYPTO IN ACL2
cells.append(md("## 9.6 Verified Cryptography in ACL2\n\nLet's survey the major verified cryptographic projects in ACL2:"))

cells.append(md("### SHA-256 Verification (`books/projects/sha-2/`)\n\nThe SHA-2 family (SHA-256, SHA-512) was formally specified and verified in ACL2.\n\n**What was proved:**\n- The implementation matches the NIST FIPS 180-4 standard\n- Correct handling of message padding and length encoding\n- Correct computation of all 64 rounds of compression\n\n**Significance:** SHA-256 is used in TLS, SSH, Bitcoin, git, and digital certificates. Having a verified reference implementation provides high assurance."))

cells.append(md("### AES Verification (`books/projects/aes/`)\n\nThe **Advanced Encryption Standard** (AES) is the most widely used symmetric cipher. The ACL2 verification covers:\n\n- All key sizes (128, 192, 256 bits)\n- Key expansion correctness\n- Round function correctness (SubBytes, ShiftRows, MixColumns, AddRoundKey)\n- Encrypt/decrypt are inverses: $D_k(E_k(m)) = m$"))

cells.append(md("### Elliptic Curve Cryptography (`books/kestrel/crypto/ecurve/`)\n\nElliptic curve cryptography (ECC) underlies modern key exchange and digital signatures (ECDSA, Ed25519). The ACL2 library verifies:\n\n- Point addition and doubling on elliptic curves\n- Curve arithmetic laws (associativity, commutativity)\n- Specific curve parameters (e.g., secp256k1 used in Bitcoin)\n\n### x86 Crypto Instructions\n\nThe `books/projects/x86isa/` project includes models of x86 instructions like AES-NI, allowing verification that **hardware crypto instructions** compute the correct results."))

# 7. EXERCISES
cells.append(md("## 9.7 Exercises"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 9.1: Modular Exponentiation Properties\n\nProve that `mod-exp` satisfies these properties:\n1. $a^0 \\equiv 1 \\pmod{n}$ (for $n > 1$)\n2. $a^1 \\equiv a \\pmod{n}$\n3. $1^e \\equiv 1 \\pmod{n}$"))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 9.1: Prove mod-exp properties\n\n; (a) a^0 ≡ 1 (mod n)\n; (thm\n;   (implies (and (natp a) (posp n) (> n 1))\n;            (equal (mod-exp a 0 n) 1)))\n\n; (b) a^1 ≡ a mod n\n; (thm\n;   (implies (and (natp a) (posp n))\n;            (equal (mod-exp a 1 n) (mod a n))))\n\n; (c) 1^e ≡ 1 mod n\n; (thm\n;   (implies (and (natp e) (posp n) (> n 1))\n;            (equal (mod-exp 1 e n) 1)))"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 9.2: Primality Testing\n\nDefine a simple (trial division) primality tester and verify it classifies some known primes and composites correctly."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 9.2: Primality testing\n;\n; (defun has-divisor (n d)\n;   ;; Check if n has a divisor between 2 and d\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; (defun primep (n)\n;   ;; n is prime iff n > 1 and has no divisor from 2 to n-1\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; Tests:\n; (primep 2)   → t\n; (primep 17)  → t\n; (primep 15)  → nil\n; (primep 1)   → nil"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 9.3: Hash Collision Detection\n\nOur `simple-hash` with a small modulus will have many collisions. Write a function that searches for a collision: two different inputs that produce the same hash."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 9.3: Find a hash collision\n;\n; (defun find-collision (n modulus)\n;   ;; Try inputs (list 0), (list 1), ..., (list n)\n;   ;; and find two that hash to the same value.\n;   YOUR DEFINITION)\n;\n; With modulus = 100, a collision is guaranteed within 101 inputs\n; (by the pigeonhole principle)"))

cells.append(md("### Exercise 9.4: Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange\n\nModel the Diffie-Hellman key exchange protocol:\n1. Public parameters: prime $p$, generator $g$\n2. Alice picks secret $a$, sends $g^a \\bmod p$\n3. Bob picks secret $b$, sends $g^b \\bmod p$\n4. Shared secret: $g^{ab} \\bmod p$\n\nShow that both sides compute the same shared secret."))

cells.append(code("; Exercise 9.4: Diffie-Hellman\n;\n; Choose small parameters for testing:\n; p = 23, g = 5, a = 6, b = 15\n;\n; Alice's public value: g^a mod p\n; Bob's public value: g^b mod p\n; Alice's shared secret: (Bob's public)^a mod p\n; Bob's shared secret: (Alice's public)^b mod p\n;\n; Verify both compute the same shared secret:\n; (let* ((p 23) (g 5) (a 6) (b 15)\n;        (alice-pub (mod-exp g a p))\n;        (bob-pub   (mod-exp g b p))\n;        (alice-secret (mod-exp bob-pub a p))\n;        (bob-secret   (mod-exp alice-pub b p)))\n;   (equal alice-secret bob-secret))"))

cells.append(md("---\n\n**Navigation:**\n[< Module 8 — SAT Solving and Boolean Reasoning](08_sat_boolean.ipynb) | [Module 10 — Capstone >](10_capstone.ipynb)"))
